=== NOTES 2:0 | Constitutional Evolution – Regulating Act 1773 to the Government of India Act 1935
## SUB 0 | Government of India Act, 1935 – Federation, Provincial Autonomy and the Constitution's Blueprint
[0:0:0] # Basis and size
[0:0:1] - **Based on:** the **Simon Commission** report, the **Round Table Conferences** (the third RTC) and the **White Paper (1933)**.
[0:0:2] - **Size:** **321 sections, 10 schedules** (14 parts) – the **longest Act** ever passed by the British Parliament up to then (APPSC JL 2001).
[0:0:3] - **Nehru's verdict:** a **"Charter of Slavery"** / "a machine with strong brakes but no engine" (UKPSC 2007).
[0:0:4] # Federation (never formed)
[0:0:5] - **All-India Federation** of British provinces + princely states – the first provision for a **federal polity** in India; the Act's own name for it = **"Federation of India"** (Part II, s.5).
[0:0:6] - **Never came into being:** joining was **optional** for the princes and the **rulers of princely states did not consent** to join (APPSC JL 2003).
[0:0:7] - **Real British intention** in bringing the princes in: to use them to **counter-balance the nationalist leaders** (UPSC 2002).
[0:0:8] - **Three lists:** **Federal (59 items), Provincial (54), Concurrent (36)** – first division of legislative powers between Centre and provinces; the basis of today's Centre–State scheme.
[0:0:9] - **Residuary powers → the Governor-General** (not the federal or provincial legislature, not the Governors) (UPSC 2018).
[0:0:10] - **Dyarchy at the Centre** proposed: **defence and foreign affairs** = reserved subjects under the **Governor-General** – **never implemented**.
[0:0:11] - **Bicameral Centre (proposed):** **Federal Assembly + Council of States**.
[0:0:12] # Provinces
[0:0:13] - **Provincial autonomy** = the **most important feature**; it **abolished provincial dyarchy** (of 1919) and gave responsible ministries from **April 1937**.
[0:0:14] - **Bicameral legislatures in 6 of 11 provinces:** **Bengal, Bombay, Madras, Bihar, Assam, United Provinces** – **not Punjab**.
[0:0:15] - **Governors kept** special powers: **veto**, ordinances and power to **legislate on their own** (Governor's Acts).
[0:0:16] - **Communal representation extended** (not abolished) to **depressed classes, women and labour**.
[0:0:17] - **Burma separated** from India; **Sindh** carved from **Bombay**, **Orissa** from **Bihar**.
[0:0:18] - **Franchise** extended to about **14%** of the population (about 3.5 crore voters); still **no adult franchise**.
[0:0:19] - **1937 elections:** Congress formed ministries in most provinces but **not in Punjab** (Unionist Party) or Bengal.
[0:0:20] - **⚠ Trap:** the 1935 Act did **NOT** bring dyarchy at **both** Centre and provinces – it **ended** provincial dyarchy and only **proposed** central dyarchy.
[0:0:21] # Institutions
[0:0:22] - **Federal Court** (Delhi) – inaugurated **1 October 1937**; first CJ **Sir Maurice Gwyer**; replaced by the Supreme Court in **1950**.
[0:0:23] - **RBI** set up on **1 April 1935** under the **RBI Act, 1934**; **Federal, Provincial and Joint PSCs** provided (no "All-India Services Commission").
[0:0:24] - **Council of India abolished**; the Secretary of State got a team of advisers instead.
[0:0:25] # Legacy in the Constitution
[0:0:26] - **Main source** of the Constitution: about **250 provisions / "nearly 75%"** drawn from it – federal scheme, office of Governor, judiciary, PSCs, emergency provisions, administrative details.
[0:0:27] - **Section 42 → Article 123** (President's ordinance power – a relic of the 1935 Act); **Section 63 → Article 175** (Governor's address).
[0:0:28] - **Centre–State relations** pattern traced to the **1935 Act** (not the US Constitution or the Nehru Report).
[0:0:29] > Statement angle (UPSC 2004): "abolition of diarchy in Governors' provinces" ✓; "Governors' power to veto and legislate" ✓; "abolition of communal representation" ✗ → **1 and 2 only**.
[0:0:30] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.97; Rocket Polity #1 p.2, #22 p.52-53; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.1 (GoI Act 1935); app notes 2:0 sub 7
## SUB 1 | Charter Acts 1793–1853 – From Trading Company to Administrator
[0:1:0] TABLE head: Act | Key provisions
  [0:1:0.0] 1793 | Monopoly extended 20 years; override power extended to all later GGs and Presidency Governors; more control over Bombay and Madras; Board of Control salaries charged to Indian revenues; C-in-C not a council member
  [0:1:0.1] 1813 | Trade monopoly in India ended except tea and China trade; Christian missionaries allowed; Rs 1 lakh a year for education (first such provision); first asserted Crown sovereignty over Company territories
  [0:1:0.2] 1833 (Saint Helena Act) | GG of Bengal → Governor-General of India (William Bentinck – last GG of Bengal, first GG of India); Company's commercial functions ended – purely administrative; Law Member added (council to 4) – Macaulay; Law Commission under Macaulay; legislation centralised; Section 87: no Indian debarred by religion, birth, descent or colour; GG-in-Council to mitigate and abolish slavery
  [0:1:0.3] 1853 (last Charter Act) | Legislative and executive functions separated; 6 legislative members added – Indian (Central) Legislative Council, the first legislature / "mini-parliament"; open competition for civil services (Macaulay Committee 1854); territories held "in trust for Her Majesty", no fixed term
[0:1:1] # Charter Act, 1813
[0:1:2] - **Ended the trade monopoly** in India (MPPSC 2004; UKPSC 2016) – but kept **tea and China** trade.
[0:1:3] - **Christian missionary activity** permitted (Charles Grant, Wilberforce).
[0:1:4] - **English education chain:** **1813** grant of Rs 1 lakh → **General Committee of Public Instruction (1823)** → **Orientalist–Anglicist controversy** → Macaulay's Minute and **Bentinck's English Education Act (1835)** – all three led to English education (UPSC 2018).
[0:1:5] # Charter Act, 1833 – most asked
[0:1:6] - **Governor-General of Bengal designated Governor-General of India** – first GG of India **Bentinck** (1833) (asked 5 times: UPSC 2023, G2, SI 2012, UKPSC 2007).
[0:1:7] - **Final step towards centralisation**; GG-in-Council got **all law-making powers** for the whole of British India – often called the **beginning of constitution-making** under British rule.
[0:1:8] - **Law Member:** **Macaulay** (first) – the **Father of the Indian Penal Code** (Law Commission draft; IPC enacted 1860).
[0:1:9] - **Slavery:** the Act enjoined the government to take measures to **abolish slavery** and improve the condition of slaves (→ Slavery Act V of **1843**).
[0:1:10] - **⚠ Trap:** the 1833 Act did **NOT** provide that an **Indian** be the Law Member (UPSC 2003); first Law Member = Macaulay, not Impey.
[0:1:11] # Charter Act, 1853
[0:1:12] - **First official legislature:** the separate Legislative Council of 1853 (APPSC TWO 2012).
[0:1:13] - **Civil services:** appointments were by **patronage of the Directors till 1853**; the 1853 Act threw them open to **competition** (exam from **1855**).
[0:1:14] - **Order:** Regulating Act 1773 → Pitt's India Act 1784 → Charter Acts (1793 onwards) → Indian Councils Act 1861 (CDS 2000).
[0:1:15] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.88-91; Rocket Polity #1 p.2, #22 p.52, #23 p.55; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.1 (Charter Act 1833); app notes 2:0 sub 2
[0:1:16] - **IPC dates:** drafted by Macaulay's First Law Commission (1837); **enacted 6 Oct 1860**; **came into force 1 Jan 1862**.
## SUB 2 | Government of India Act, 1919 – Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms
[0:2:0] # Basis
[0:2:1] - **Montagu's declaration, 20 August 1917:** "increasing association of Indians in every branch of administration" and gradual **responsible government**; **Montagu-Chelmsford Report 1918**.
[0:2:2] - **Edwin Montagu** = Secretary of State; **Lord Chelmsford** = Viceroy.
[0:2:3] # Provisions
[0:2:4] - **Dyarchy in the provinces** (Greek *di-arche*, double rule) – first time; provincial subjects split into **reserved** and **transferred** (MPPSC 2022; UPSC 2017).
[0:2:5] - **Reserved subjects:** administered by the **Governor** with his **Executive Council** (not answerable to the legislature).
[0:2:6] - **Transferred subjects:** Governor with **Indian ministers** responsible to the legislative council.
[0:2:7] - **Central and provincial subjects** separated – it **clearly defined the jurisdiction of the central and provincial governments** (UPSC 2015); **budgets separated** for the first time.
[0:2:8] - **Bicameral central legislature** first time: **Council of State** (upper, 60) + **Legislative Assembly** (lower, **145**: 104 elected + 41 nominated); **direct elections** on a limited franchise (property, tax, education) (asked 4 times).
[0:2:9] - Term **"responsible government"** first used; 3 of the 6 members of the Viceroy's Executive Council to be Indians.
[0:2:10] - **Communal representation extended** to **Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans**.
[0:2:11] - **High Commissioner for India in London**; **Public Service Commission** provided – set up **1 October 1926** (Chairman + 4) (UPPSC 2008); **Public Accounts Committee 1921**.
[0:2:12] - **Civil services:** the ICS examination to be held **in India** too (from 1922); by **1941** Indians outnumbered Europeans in the ICS (UPRO 2021: both statements correct).
[0:2:13] - **Chamber of Princes**: **120** members, King George V's Proclamation **23 December 1919**; first Chancellor **Ganga Singh of Bikaner** (UPPSC 2023).
[0:2:14] - **Statutory commission after 10 years** → **Simon Commission** appointed **1927** (two years early), 7 British members under **Sir John Simon**; "Simon Go Back".
[0:2:15] - **Last election** of the Indian (central) legislature under this Act: **1945**.
[0:2:16] # Reserved vs Transferred
[0:2:17] TABLE head: Reserved (Governor + Executive Council) | Transferred (Governor + Indian Ministers)
  [0:2:17.0] Police, administration of justice, land revenue, law and order, irrigation, finance | Local self-government, education, public health, agriculture, public works
[0:2:18] > UPSC 2022: reserved = justice, land revenue, police → **1, 3 and 4**; local self-government was transferred.
[0:2:19] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.96; Rocket Polity #1 p.2, p.4-5, #22 p.52; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.1 (GoI Act 1919); en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Legislative_Assembly (145 members); app notes 2:0 sub 6
[0:2:20] - **First elections** to the reformed (Montford) councils: **November 1920** (boycotted by the Congress during Non-Cooperation); the new legislatures met in 1921.
## SUB 3 | Crown Rule – Government of India Act, 1858
[0:3:0] - **Rule transferred from the East India Company to the British Crown** – the main administrative consequence of the **Revolt of 1857**; British India under **direct Crown rule from 1858** (asked 8 times).
[0:3:1] - **"Act for the Good Government of India"**; Parliament passed it for a more responsible management of Indian affairs after the revolt exposed Company misrule – **not** because the Company was unwilling to rule (NDA 2006).
[0:3:2] - **Governor-General also styled Viceroy**: **Lord Canning** first; last Viceroy **Mountbatten**.
[0:3:3] - **Board of Control and Court of Directors abolished** – dual government ended (**⚠** "retained the Court of Directors" is the wrong statement).
[0:3:4] - **Secretary of State for India** created – a member of the British cabinet with a **15-member Council of India**; he **issued instructions to the Governor-General** and the Crown's powers were exercised through him.
[0:3:5] - **Queen Victoria's Proclamation (1 Nov 1858)** – called the **"Magna Carta of Indian liberties"** (**⚠** not the Charter Act 1813).
[0:3:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.92; Rocket Polity #1 p.2, #22 p.52; app notes 2:0 sub 3
## SUB 4 | Regulating Act 1773, Pitt's India Act 1784 and the Act of 1786
[0:4:0] # Background
[0:4:1] - **Act of 1767:** the Company to pay the British Government **£400,000 a year** (APPSC G2 2017).
[0:4:2] - **1772 crisis:** famine, falling revenue, **corruption and oppression by Company officials** (also in justice), loan of about £1 million sought → Parliament intervened; **Lord North** committee report.
[0:4:3] - **Before 1773:** the head of Fort William was the **Governor of Bengal** (1757–1772).
[0:4:4] # Regulating Act, 1773
[0:4:5] - **First** step by Parliament to **control** the Company – British Government's control increased; it **altered the Company's constitution at home** (Court of Directors, Court of Proprietors) and its structure in India.
[0:4:6] - **Governor of Bengal → Governor-General of Bengal**: **Warren Hastings** (first), with a **4-member Executive Council**.
[0:4:7] - **Bombay and Madras made subordinate** to Bengal (Calcutta).
[0:4:8] - **Supreme Court at Calcutta (Fort William)** – provided by the Act, set up by Charter in **1774**: **1 Chief Justice + 3 judges**; first CJ **Sir Elijah Impey** (asked 4 times).
[0:4:9] - **Banned private trade and bribes** by Company servants.
[0:4:10] - Directors to send letters on revenue, civil and military affairs to the British Government.
[0:4:11] - **⚠ Trap:** it did **NOT abolish the Court of Directors** – both it and the Board of Control went only in **1858** (APPSC 2019).
[0:4:12] - **Amending Act 1781 (Act of Settlement):** separated judicial and executive functions.
[0:4:13] # Pitt's India Act, 1784 and the Act of 1786
[0:4:14] - **Dual control / double government:** **Board of Control** (political, **6 members**) + **Court of Directors** (commercial).
[0:4:15] - Company territories called **"British possessions in India"**; GG's council cut to **3**.
[0:4:16] - **Act of 1786:** GG may **override his Council** and act as **Commander-in-Chief** – first used by **Lord Cornwallis**.
[0:4:17] > Match (UPSC 2002): 1813 – monopoly ended; Regulating Act – directors to submit correspondence; 1858 – power to the Crown; Pitt's Act – Board of Control → **A-2, B-4, C-3, D-1**.
[0:4:18] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.81-87; Rocket Polity #1 p.2, #22 p.52; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.1 (Regulating Act); app notes 2:0 sub 0-1
## SUB 5 | Indian Councils Acts 1861 and 1892 – Ordinances, Portfolios, Indirect Election
[0:5:0] # Indian Councils Act, 1861
[0:5:1] - **Restored legislative powers of Bombay and Madras** (decentralisation); new councils in Bengal (1862), NWFP (1866), Punjab (1897).
[0:5:2] - **Beginning of representative institutions:** Canning nominated 3 Indians as non-officials – **Raja of Benaras, Maharaja of Patiala, Sir Dinkar Rao** (not Sir Syed Ahmad Khan, who criticised the absence of Indians as a lesson of 1857).
[0:5:3] - **Portfolio system** (begun by Canning, 1859) got statutory recognition – the Viceroy's authority over his council strengthened (UPSC 2002).
[0:5:4] - **Ordinance power** to the Viceroy in emergency – valid at most **six months** (UPPSC 2001).
[0:5:5] - **Indian High Courts Act 1861** → High Courts of **Calcutta, Bombay, Madras (1862)**.
[0:5:6] # Indian Councils Act, 1892
[0:5:7] - **Indirect election** introduced (asked 4 times): nomination on the recommendation of **universities, district boards, municipalities, chambers of commerce**.
[0:5:8] - **Budget discussion** allowed (no vote); questions with **6 days' notice**, **no supplementary** questions.
[0:5:9] TABLE head: Feature | 1861 | 1892
  [0:5:9.0] Indians | Nominated non-officials | More non-officials
  [0:5:9.1] Election | None | Indirect begins
  [0:5:9.2] Budget | No discussion | Discussion, no vote
  [0:5:9.3] Other | Portfolio, ordinance | Right to ask questions
[0:5:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.93-94; Rocket Polity #22 p.52, #23 p.115; app notes 2:0 sub 4
## SUB 6 | Indian Councils Act, 1909 – Morley-Minto Reforms
[0:6:0] - **John Morley** = Secretary of State; **Lord Minto** = Viceroy – **"Father of Communal Electorate"**.
[0:6:1] - **Separate electorate for Muslims** = **communal representation** (asked 4 times: SI 2011; MPPSC 2017, 2019).
[0:6:2] - **Elected representation:** the Act that "provided for some elected representation" = **1909** (APPSC 2020); often called the **first attempt to introduce a representative and popular element** (SSC 2000).
[0:6:3] - **Indians associated with the executive councils** for the first time: **S.P. Sinha** = first Indian in the Viceroy's Executive Council, as **Law Member** – the first "closer association of Indians with the administration".
[0:6:4] - Central council: additional members raised to **60** (total **69**); **official majority** at the Centre, non-official majority in provinces; still **unicameral**.
[0:6:5] - **Supplementary questions**, resolutions and budget discussion allowed.
[0:6:6] - **⚠ Trap:** no dyarchy and no Chamber of Princes in 1909 (both 1919); indirect election was 1892, not 1909.
[0:6:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.95; Rocket Polity #1 p.2, #22 p.52; en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Councils_Act_1909; app notes 2:0 sub 5
## SUB 7 | Allied Facts Asked with This Topic
[0:7:0] - **Local self-government:** **Lord Ripon** – Resolution of **1882**, the "Magna Carta of local self-government" (not Canning or Macaulay).
[0:7:1] - **Fundamental rights demand:** first in the **Constitution of India Bill, 1895**; the **Congress (Bombay special session, 1918)** demanded a **Declaration of Rights**; then Nehru Report 1928, Karachi 1931.
[0:7:2] - **Muslims and the Congress:** **Badruddin Tyabji** (Congress president 1887) appealed to Muslims to join the Congress; Sir Syed Ahmad Khan opposed it.
[0:7:3] - **Chronology:** INC formed **1885** → Simon Commission **1927** → Round Table Conferences **1930–32** → Quit India **1942**.
[0:7:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.399 (Ripon); Rocket Polity #1 p.3 (1895 Bill); inc.in brief history (1918 Bombay session); app notes 2:0
[0:7:5] - **Book:** *Landmarks in Indian Constitutional and National Development (1600-1919)* = **Gurmukh Nihal Singh** (later first Governor of Rajasthan).
## SUB 8 | Designations, Dyarchy and Firsts – Comparison
[0:8:0] TABLE head: Designation | Act | First holder
  [0:8:0.0] Governor-General of Bengal | 1773 | Warren Hastings
  [0:8:0.1] Governor-General of India | 1833 | William Bentinck
  [0:8:0.2] Viceroy | 1858 | Lord Canning
[0:8:1] TABLE head: Feature | 1909 | 1919 / 1935
  [0:8:1.0] Dyarchy | None | Provinces 1919; Centre proposed 1935 (never worked)
  [0:8:1.1] Central legislature | Unicameral | Bicameral 1919; Federal (proposed) 1935
  [0:8:1.2] Communal representation | Muslims | Extended 1919; further extended 1935
[0:8:2] - **Elections:** indirect **1892** → separate electorate **1909** → direct **1919**.
[0:8:3] - **Bicameral:** Centre **1919**; provinces (6) **1935**.
[0:8:4] - **Ordinance:** Viceroy's power from **1861**; Art. 123 modelled on s.42 of **1935**.
[0:8:5] - **Monopoly:** partly ended **1813**, wholly **1833**; last Charter Act **1853**; Court of Directors abolished **1858**.
[0:8:6] TABLE head: Number | Fact
  [0:8:6.0] 1773 council / 1784 council | 4 / 3
  [0:8:6.1] Supreme Court 1774 | CJ + 3 judges
  [0:8:6.2] Education grant 1813 | Rs 1 lakh
  [0:8:6.3] 1853 legislative members | 6
  [0:8:6.4] Council of India | 15
  [0:8:6.5] 1909 additional members | 60 (total 69)
  [0:8:6.6] Chamber of Princes | 120
  [0:8:6.7] 1935 bicameral provinces | 6
  [0:8:6.8] 1935 Act | 321 sections, 10 schedules
[0:8:7] ~~ Sources: Rocket Polity #22 p.52-54, #23 p.55, p.115; NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.81-98; app notes 2:0 sub 8-9

=== OWN MCQs of 2:0 (123)
[nindian-polity-1-27] sub0
  Who was the first Chief Justice of the Federal Court (inaugurated on 1 October 1937)?
  Sir Patrick Spens | *Sir Maurice Gwyer | H.J. Kania | Sir Elijah Impey
  x: Sir Maurice Gwyer was the first Chief Justice of the Federal Court, which the Supreme Court replaced in 1950.
[n1abbf33ea6e] sub0
  Which of the following provinces was NOT among the six provinces provided with bicameral legislatures under the Government of India Act, 1935?
  Assam | Bihar | United Provinces | *Punjab
  x: Bengal, Bombay, Madras, Bihar, Assam and the United Provinces had bicameral legislatures. Punjab was not included in this group.
[n28fe9c75572] sub0
  How many sections did the Government of India Act, 1935 contain?
  395 | 231 | *321 | 312
  x: The Government of India Act, 1935 had 321 sections, 14 parts and 10 schedules.
[n71d595c1b39] sub0
  Which provision of the Government of India Act, 1935 was never implemented?
  Provincial autonomy | *Dyarchy at the Centre | Federal Court at Delhi | Federal and Provincial Public Service Commissions
  x: Dyarchy at the Centre was never implemented because the Federation did not come into operation; provincial autonomy and the Federal Court were implemented.
[ne643f048b2b] sub0
  From when did responsible governments in the provinces under the Government of India Act, 1935 come into force?
  April 1935 | April 1936 | *April 1937 | January 1939
  x: The 1935 Act abolished provincial dyarchy, and ministries answerable to the legislatures took office from April 1937.
[nf25ad982f52] sub0
  On which discussions was the Government of India Act, 1935 primarily based?
  First Round Table Conference and the Simon Commission Report | *Third Round Table Conference and the White Paper | Second Round Table Conference and the Nehru Report | Cabinet Mission and the Cripps Proposals
  x: The Government of India Act, 1935 was based on the Third Round Table Conference and the White Paper.
[n62057d4fb55] sub0
  Why did the All India Federation proposed by the Government of India Act, 1935 never come into existence?
  The provinces rejected it | The Federal Court declared it invalid | Parliament repealed the federal part | *No princely state joined it
  x: Accession of the princely states was optional, and since no princely state joined, the Federation never came into operation.
[n74214746fc9] sub0
  Under the Government of India Act, 1935, what was the position of Defence and Foreign Affairs?
  Federal subjects under the federal legislature | *Reserved subjects under the Governor-General | Transferred subjects under federal ministers | Concurrent subjects
  x: Defence and foreign affairs were reserved subjects under the Governor-General, not the federal legislature.
[nc3bbd12defc] sub0
  Under the Government of India Act, 1935, the central legislature was to consist of the Federal Assembly and which other House?
  *Council of States | Council of State | Chamber of Princes | Council of India
  x: The 1935 Act's bicameral Centre was the Federal Assembly plus the Council of States; communal representation was also extended to depressed classes, women and labour.
[n350222ac172] sub0
  Under the Government of India Act, 1935, from which province was Sindh separated?
  Punjab | Bengal | Madras | *Bombay
  x: The 1935 Act separated Burma from India, Sindh from Bombay and Orissa from Bihar.
[n36a9a6287ab] sub0
  Which one of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched in relation to the Government of India Act, 1935?
  Residuary powers — Governor-General | *Council of India — Continued without alteration | Federal Court — Delhi | Dyarchy in provinces — Abolished
  x: The Council of India created under the Government of India Act, 1858 was abolished, and the Secretary of State was provided with advisers.
[ne84b0d4540b] sub0
  Which one of the following provisions of the Government of India Act, 1935 was borrowed into the Constitution of India in substantially corresponding form?
  The non-operational central dyarchy | The separate colonial status of Burma | *The federal scheme | The compulsory accession of princely states
  x: The federal scheme, office of Governor, judiciary, Public Service Commissions, emergency provisions and administrative details were among the features carried into the Constitution. Compulsory accession and non-operational dyarchy were not borrowed as constitutional arrangements.
[n6763e97a1c4] sub0
  Under which Act was the Reserve Bank of India formed on 1 April 1935?
  Government of India Act, 1935 | Government of India Act, 1919 | Banking Regulation Act, 1949 | *RBI Act, 1934
  x: The 1935 Act provided for the RBI, but the bank was formed on 1 April 1935 under the RBI Act, 1934.
[n8deab8e3fc1] sub0
  Article 175 of the Constitution (Governor's address) corresponds to which Section of the Government of India Act, 1935?
  *Section 63 | Section 42 | Section 87 | Section 72
  x: Section 63 of the 1935 Act dealt with the Governor's address, now Article 175; Section 42 is the ordinance model for Article 123.
[nindian-polity-22-33] sub0
  Which Section of the Government of India Act, 1935 is the model for the President's ordinance power under Article 123?
  Section 72 | *Section 42 | Section 63 | Section 87
  x: Section 42 of the 1935 Act is the model for Article 123; the Act's All-India Federation and Central Dyarchy were never implemented.
[f4b33f0530d46] sub0
  In which year was the Federal Court set up under the 1935 Act replaced by the Supreme Court?
  1948 | *1950 | 1947 | 1937
  x: The Federal Court, inaugurated in 1937, was replaced by the Supreme Court in 1950.
[fdd677481cdcf] sub0
  To about what share of the population did the Government of India Act, 1935 extend the franchise?
  10% | 25% | *14% | 3%
  x: The franchise rose from about 3% to about 14% of the population (about 3.5 crore voters).
[f3134b0e9bb37] sub0
  To which groups did the Government of India Act, 1935 extend communal representation?
  Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians and Europeans | Muslims only | Muslims and Sikhs only | *Depressed classes, women and labour
  x: The 1935 Act extended communal representation to depressed classes, women and labour; the 1919 Act had covered Sikhs, Christians, Anglo-Indians and Europeans.
[f9bfa792126b4] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the Government of India Act, 1935?
  *Defence and Foreign Affairs were placed under the federal legislature. | It proposed an All-India Federation. | It provided bicameral legislatures in six provinces. | It vested residuary powers in the Governor-General.
  x: Defence and Foreign Affairs were reserved subjects under the Governor-General, not the federal legislature.
[fcf155ccc1394] sub0
  Under the Government of India Act, 1935, what happened to the Council of India?
  Made fully elective | *Abolished | Expanded to 15 members | Merged with the Board of Control
  x: The 1935 Act abolished the Council of India and gave the Secretary of State advisers instead.
[gbf59ea9ac83c] sub0
  Which Act separated Burma from British India?
  Government of India Act, 1919 | *Government of India Act, 1935 | Indian Councils Act, 1909 | Indian Independence Act, 1947
  x: The Government of India Act, 1935 separated Burma from India and also created Sindh and Orissa as provinces.

  In which year was the Federal Court, set up under the Government of India Act, 1935, inaugurated?

  Charter Act, 1833 | *Indian Councils Act, 1861 | Indian Councils Act, 1892 | Indian Councils Act, 1909
  x: The Indian Councils Act, 1861 restored the legislative powers of Bombay and Madras; separate electorates came only in 1909.
[f6854526f6710] sub5
  Under the Indian Councils Act, 1892, which of the following could the legislative councils do?
  *Discuss the budget but not vote on it | Vote on the budget | Ask supplementary questions | Not discuss the budget at all
  x: The 1892 Act allowed discussion of the budget without a vote; supplementary questions came only in 1909.
[f1e0b6d7e67cc] sub5
  Under the Indian Councils Act, 1892, how many days' notice did questions in the councils require?
  15 days | *6 days | 3 days | 10 days
  x: Questions needed 6 days' notice, and no supplementary questions were allowed.
[f000e52dae43a] sub5
  Which of the following was NOT a feature of the Indian Councils Act, 1861?
  It gave statutory recognition to the portfolio system. | It gave the Viceroy an ordinance power of up to six months. | It restored the legislative powers of Bombay and Madras. | *It began indirect election to the councils.
  x: Indirect election began only with the Indian Councils Act, 1892.
[n14fb65bdd0a] sub6
  Under the Indian Councils Act, 1909, to how many were the additional members of the Central Legislative Council raised?
  16 | 32 | *60 | 100
  x: The 1909 Act raised the additional members to 60 (total 69), though the Centre kept an official majority.
[n75af0835a86] sub6
  At the time of the Morley-Minto Reforms (1909), which posts did Morley and Minto respectively hold?
  Viceroy and Secretary of State | *Secretary of State and Viceroy | Governor of Bombay and Secretary of State | Secretary of State and Governor of Bengal
  x: John Morley was Secretary of State for India and Lord Minto was the Viceroy when the Indian Councils Act, 1909 was passed.
[nindian-polity-1-24] sub6
  Who is known as the 'Father of Communal Electorate'?
  Lord Morley | Lord Chelmsford | *Lord Minto | Lord Curzon
  x: Lord Minto, of the Morley-Minto reforms (Indian Councils Act, 1909), which introduced separate electorates for Muslims.
[nindian-polity-1-23] sub6
  Who became the first Indian Law Member of the Viceroy's Executive Council after the 1909 Act?
  Sir Syed Ahmad Khan | Maharaja Ganga Singh | *S.P. Sinha | Sir Dinkar Rao
  x: The 1909 Act first brought Indians into the Viceroy's and Governors' Executive Councils, and S.P. Sinha became the first Indian Law Member.
[fae66198010f6] sub6
  Which Act first allowed supplementary questions in the legislative councils?
  Indian Councils Act, 1892 | Indian Councils Act, 1861 | Government of India Act, 1919 | *Indian Councils Act, 1909
  x: The 1892 Act allowed no supplementary questions; the 1909 Act allowed them.
[f70736121e635] sub6
  Under the Indian Councils Act, 1909, what was the total strength of the Central Legislative Council?
  *69 | 60 | 37 | 32
  x: Additional members rose to 60, making a total of 69 (37 officials and 32 non-officials).
[gd442a913156b] sub6
  Which Act introduced separate electorates for Muslims?
  *Indian Councils Act, 1909 | Indian Councils Act, 1892 | Government of India Act, 1919 | Government of India Act, 1935
  x: The Morley-Minto Reforms (Indian Councils Act, 1909) gave Muslims a separate electorate; Lord Minto is called the Father of Communal Electorate.
[g7f305af273e9] sub6
  Consider the following statements about the Indian Councils Act, 1909:
1. It kept an official majority in the Central Legislative Council.
2. It allowed a non-official majority in the provincial councils.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | 2 only | *Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: The 1909 Act kept an official majority at the Centre but allowed a non-official majority in the provinces.
[nindian-polity-22-31] sub6
  Which of the following pairs is wrongly matched?
  Satyendra Prasad Sinha – First Indian on the Viceroy's Executive Council | Government of India Act, 1919 – Chamber of Princes | Government of India Act, 1935 – Maurice Gwyer as Chief Justice of the Federal Court | *Indian Councils Act, 1909 – Established the Chamber of Princes
  x: The Chamber of Princes was set up under the 1919 Act; the 1909 Act allowed supplementary questions.
[nindian-polity-43-4] sub6
  Which of the following statements is incorrect?
  The Charter Act, 1833 added a Law Member and ended the Company's commercial activities. | The Indian Councils Act, 1892 permitted budget discussion and limited indirect election. | *The Morley-Minto Reforms introduced dyarchy in the provinces. | The Government of India Act, 1858 transferred power to the British Crown.
  x: C is incorrect: provincial dyarchy came with the Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (1919), not the Morley-Minto Reforms (1909), which brought separate electorates. The other three statements are correct.
[nac3228e7d4c] sub8
  Who was the first Governor-General of India?
  Lord Cornwallis | *William Bentinck | Lord Canning | Warren Hastings
  x: William Bentinck became the first Governor-General of India under the Charter Act of 1833; Canning was the first Viceroy and Hastings the first Governor-General of Bengal.
[f4dbe444b2ccf] sub8
  Which of the following designation – first holder pairs is correctly matched?
  Viceroy – Warren Hastings | Viceroy – William Bentinck | *Governor-General of India – William Bentinck | Governor-General of Bengal – Lord Cornwallis
  x: Bentinck was the first Governor-General of India (1833); Hastings was the first Governor-General of Bengal and Canning the first Viceroy.
[ga2cb7c3b426b] sub8
  Match the following:
List-I (Designation)
A. Governor-General of Bengal
B. Governor-General of India
C. Viceroy
List-II (Act)
1. Charter Act, 1833
2. Regulating Act, 1773
3. Government of India Act, 1858
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
  *A-2, B-1, C-3 | A-1, B-2, C-3 | A-2, B-3, C-1 | A-3, B-1, C-2
  x: GG of Bengal came by the Regulating Act 1773, GG of India by the Charter Act 1833, and the Viceroy title by the GoI Act 1858.
[nindian-history-92-6] sub8
  Which of the following statements is incorrect?
  The Government of India Act, 1858 ended Company rule. | The Indian Councils Act, 1861 associated Indians with legislative councils. | *The Government of India Act, 1858 introduced dyarchy in provinces. | The Charter Act, 1853 was the last Charter Act.
  x: Option C is incorrect: dyarchy in the provinces came with the Government of India Act, 1919, not 1858. The 1858 Act ended Company rule, and the other statements are right.

=== NOTES 2:1 | Indian Independence Act 1947; the Constituent Assembly – Demand, Composition, Functioning
## SUB 0 | Office-bearers, Drafting Committee and the Constitutional Adviser
[1:0:0] # Presiding officers
[1:0:1] TABLE head: Office | Person
  [1:0:1.0] Temporary (provisional / pro-tem) President, first meeting 9 Dec 1946 | Dr Sachchidananda Sinha – eldest member, following the French practice
  [1:0:1.1] First (nominated) temporary Deputy Chairman | Frank Anthony – nominated by Sinha on 9 Dec 1946
  [1:0:1.2] Permanent President (elected 11 December 1946) | Dr Rajendra Prasad
  [1:0:1.3] Vice-Presidents | H.C. Mukherjee (first, elected with Prasad) and V.T. Krishnamachari
  [1:0:1.4] Constitutional (legal) Adviser | Sir B.N. Rau
  [1:0:1.5] Speaker when sitting as Dominion Legislature | G.V. Mavalankar
[1:0:2] - **"First President" of the Assembly:** the first to preside was **Sachchidananda Sinha** (temporary); the elected (permanent) President was **Rajendra Prasad** – read which one the question wants.
[1:0:3] - **Not the presiding officer:** **Nehru** (he moved the Objectives Resolution) and **Ambedkar** (he chaired only the Drafting Committee).
[1:0:4] # Constitutional Adviser – B.N. Rau
[1:0:5] - **Sir B.N. (Benegal Narsing) Rau** – **Constitutional / legal Adviser**; prepared the **first draft** in **October 1947**: **243 Articles, 13 Schedules**, placed before the Drafting Committee.
[1:0:6] - Later a judge of the **International Court of Justice** (1952-53).
[1:0:7] - **⚠ Not** Sachchidananda Sinha, K.M. Munshi, Alladi or M.C. Setalvad (Setalvad = first Attorney General).
[1:0:8] # Drafting Committee
[1:0:9] - Set up by a resolution of the Assembly on **29 August 1947**; **7 members**; sat **141 days**.
[1:0:10] - **Chairman: Dr B.R. Ambedkar** – the **"architect" / "Father" of the Constitution**, "Modern Manu".
[1:0:11] - Members: **N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar**, **Alladi Krishnaswamy Ayyar**, **K.M. Munshi**, **Mohammad Saadullah**, **B.L. Mitter** (replaced by **N. Madhava Rau**), **D.P. Khaitan** (died 1948; replaced by **T.T. Krishnamachari**).
[1:0:12] - **NOT members:** **Nehru**, **Rajendra Prasad**, **J.B. Kripalani**, **Sardar Patel**, **Vijayalakshmi Pandit**, B.N. Rau (adviser, not member).
[1:0:13] - **Preamble:** proposed before the Drafting Committee by **Jawaharlal Nehru** (it was based on his Objectives Resolution).
[1:0:14] # Artwork of the original Constitution
[1:0:15] - **Calligraphy (English):** **Prem Behari Narain Raizada** (flowing italic); Hindi copy: Vasant Krishan Vaidya.
[1:0:16] - **Illustrations:** artists of **Shantiniketan** led by **Nandalal Bose**; **National Emblem** sketched by **Dinanath Bhargava** (under Bose); Preamble page illuminated by **Beohar Rammanohar Sinha**.
[1:0:17] > Trap pairs: V.T. Krishnamachari = Vice-President, T.T. Krishnamachari = Drafting Committee; Sachchidananda Sinha = provisional President, Beohar Rammanohar Sinha = Preamble art.
[1:0:18] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.135-142, p.150-153; Rocket Polity #5 p.14-15, p.3; app notes 2:1 sub 2
[1:0:19] - **"Deshratna":** popular title of **Dr Rajendra Prasad** (Assembly President, first President of India).
## SUB 1 | Chronology – First Meeting to Commencement
[1:1:0] TABLE head: Date | Event
  [1:1:0.0] 16 May 1946 | Cabinet Mission Plan announced – scheme for the Assembly
  [1:1:0.1] July–Aug 1946 | Elections to the Assembly
  [1:1:0.2] 9 Dec 1946 | First meeting, Constitution Hall (now Central Hall of Parliament), New Delhi; 207 members (9 women) – NextGen gives 211; Muslim League boycotted; Nehru attended
  [1:1:0.3] 11 Dec 1946 | Rajendra Prasad elected permanent President
  [1:1:0.4] 13 Dec 1946 | Objectives Resolution moved by Nehru – in the fifth meeting of the first session (session ended 23 Dec 1946)
  [1:1:0.5] 22 Jan 1947 | Objectives Resolution adopted unanimously → became the Preamble
  [1:1:0.6] 28 Apr 1947 | Princely states' representatives join (third session)
  [1:1:0.7] 22 Jul 1947 | National Flag adopted
  [1:1:0.8] 14–15 Aug 1947 | Re-assembled as the sovereign Assembly of the Dominion of India
  [1:1:0.9] 29 Aug 1947 | Drafting Committee appointed
  [1:1:0.10] 17 Nov 1947 | First sat as Dominion Legislature (Speaker Mavalankar)
  [1:1:0.11] 21 Feb 1948 | Draft Constitution published
  [1:1:0.12] 4 Nov 1948 | Ambedkar moves the Draft – first reading
  [1:1:0.13] May 1949 | Commonwealth membership ratified
  [1:1:0.14] 14 Sep 1949 | Munshi-Ayyangar formula (Hindi Diwas)
  [1:1:0.15] 14 Nov 1949 | Third reading begins
  [1:1:0.16] 26 Nov 1949 | Constitution adopted and enacted (last day of 11th session) – Constitution Day
  [1:1:0.17] 24 Jan 1950 | Last (final) meeting: members sign; National Anthem and National Song adopted; Rajendra Prasad elected first President of India
  [1:1:0.18] 26 Jan 1950 | Commencement – Republic
[1:1:1] # Adoption vs commencement
[1:1:2] - **Adopted, enacted and given to ourselves** by the **Constituent Assembly** on **26 November 1949** (not by the British Parliament, the GG or Parliament) – Preamble: "this **twenty-sixth day of November, 1949**".
[1:1:3] - **In force on 26 Nov 1949:** **citizenship**, **elections**, provisional Parliament, temporary and transitional provisions (Arts. 5-9, 60, 324, 366, 367, 379, 380, 388, 391-393) – **not** emergency provisions or appointment of judges.
[1:1:4] - **Rest in force on 26 January 1950** – chosen because the Congress had observed **26 January 1930** as **Purna Swaraj (Independence) Day** (Lahore session resolution, **December 1929**, Nehru presiding) – ⚠ the resolution was not of "26 January 1929".
[1:1:5] - **Anniversaries:** 26 Nov 2019 = **70th** anniversary of adoption; Constitution Day (Samvidhan Divas) observed since **2015**.
[1:1:6] > Statement angle (Raj PCS 2023): final session 24 Jan 1950 ✓ and Prasad declared elected President of India in it ✓ → **both correct**.
[1:1:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.5, p.136-143; Rocket Polity #5 p.14, #43 p.114; app notes 2:1 sub 5
[1:1:8] - **Constitution Day (2015):** declared as part of the **125th birth anniversary year of Dr B.R. Ambedkar** (born 14 April 1891 → 125th anniversary **14 April 2016**).
## SUB 2 | Composition and Method of Election
[1:2:0] TABLE head: Item | Figure
  [1:2:0.0] Total planned strength | 389
  [1:2:0.1] British India | 296 = 292 Governor's provinces + 4 Chief Commissioners' provinces (Delhi, Ajmer-Merwara, Coorg, British Baluchistan)
  [1:2:0.2] Princely States | 93
  [1:2:0.3] Ratio | 1 seat per 10 lakh (one million) population
  [1:2:0.4] After partition | 299 (provinces 229 + states 70) – members from Pakistan areas withdrew
  [1:2:0.5] 1946 result | Congress 208, Muslim League 73 (second largest), others/independents 15
  [1:2:0.6] Women members | 15
[1:2:1] - **Provincial seats** split among **Muslims, Sikhs and General** by population.
[1:2:2] - **Indirectly elected** by the members of the **Provincial Legislative Assemblies** (set up under the 1935 Act) – each community's MLAs voting by **proportional representation with single transferable vote** (the 292 provincial members).
[1:2:3] - **NOT** directly elected, **NOT** adult franchise (restricted franchise of the **1935 Act, Sixth Schedule** – tax, property, education), not nominated by the British, the GG, the Congress/League or Gandhi.
[1:2:4] - **Princely states' representatives nominated by the rulers**; so the body was **partly elected, partly nominated**.
[1:2:5] - **Minorities** (Christians, Anglo-Indians, Parsis…) were represented; it worked through many committees.
[1:2:6] - **Not members:** **Mahatma Gandhi** and **M.A. Jinnah** (all other leading personalities were in it).
[1:2:7] - **Ambedkar** – first elected from **Bengal** (eastern part, later East Pakistan); then from the **Bombay** seat vacated by **M.R. Jayakar** → he sat for **Bombay Presidency**.
[1:2:8] - **Provinces represented:** **Patel – Bombay**, **Rajendra Prasad – Bihar**, **H.C. Mookherjee – West Bengal**, **Durgabai Deshmukh – Madras**; Bihar also sent **A.N. Sinha**, **Jagjivan Ram**, Sachchidananda Sinha.
[1:2:9] - Princely states joined from the third session (**28 April 1947**: Baroda, Bikaner, Jaipur, Patiala, Rewa, Udaipur).
[1:2:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.104-110, p.131-132, p.145; Rocket Polity #5 p.14; constitutionofindia.net members pages; app notes 2:1 sub 1
[1:2:11] - **Deferment of adult franchise for 15 years:** pleaded in the Assembly by **Maulana Azad** (exam sources); **Rajendra Prasad** and **Nehru** backed immediate adult franchise.
## SUB 3 | National Symbols Adopted by the Assembly
[1:3:0] - **National Flag** – design adopted **22 July 1947** (resolution moved by Nehru); the wheel = **Ashoka Chakra (Dharmachakra)**, **24 spokes**, the **wheel of law** – stands for **justice** (dharma).
[1:3:1] - **Flag Code of India, 2002:** ratio of length to height **3:2**; standard sizes include 900×600 mm and 450×300 mm – **600×400 mm is not** a standard size.
[1:3:2] - **Jana Gana Mana** (Tagore, originally **Bengali**) – adopted in its Hindi version as the **National Anthem** on **24 January 1950** (Prasad's statement, not a vote); full rendition **52 seconds**; first sung at the Calcutta Congress, 27 Dec 1911.
[1:3:3]   - Its regions include **Sindh**, now in **Pakistan** (Utkala, Dravida, Maratha are in India).
[1:3:4]   - **January 2017:** lakhs of devotees at **Khodaldham, Kagvad (near Rajkot)** set a **Guinness record** for **collective singing of the National Anthem**.
[1:3:5] - **Vande Mataram** (Bankimchandra) – originally in **Sanskrit** (in Bengali script), **not Bengali**; declared **National Song** on 24 Jan 1950, **honoured equally**; first political singing 1896 Congress.
[1:3:6] - **Art. 51A(a)** names the **Flag and Anthem**, not the Song; the **Prevention of Insults to National Honour (Amendment) Act, 2026** (passed July 2026) gives the Song statutory protection like the Anthem; **Bijoe Emmanuel v State of Kerala (1986)** – standing respectfully without singing is no offence.
[1:3:7] - **Satyameva Jayate** (Mundaka Upanishad) – national **motto**, adopted with the **State Emblem** on **26 January 1950**; inscribed below the emblem on currency notes and national documents.
[1:3:8] - **National Calendar (Saka):** 1 Chaitra = **22 March** (21 March in a leap year).
[1:3:9] - **National flower: Lotus** (*Nelumbo nucifera*).
[1:3:10] > UPSC 2003 NOT-correct statement: "Vande Mataram composed originally in **Bengali**" ✗ (it was Sanskrit).
[1:3:11] - **Language – Munshi-Ayyangar formula (14 Sep 1949):** **Hindi in Devanagari** as official language of the Union, **English continued for 15 years**, **14** other languages in the Eighth Schedule – **not** an immediate end of English.
[1:3:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.143, p.145-146, p.148; knowindia.india.gov.in (national symbols); mha.gov.in Flag Code of India 2002; prsindia.org (National Honour Amendment Bill 2026); guinnessworldrecords.com (largest singing of a national anthem, 2017); app notes 2:1 sub 7
[1:3:13] - **Flag colours (S. Radhakrishnan in the Assembly):** **saffron** – renunciation / courage and sacrifice; **white** – light, the path of truth / peace; **green** – our relation to the soil, the plant life (growth, fertility).
[1:3:14] - **Seal of the Constituent Assembly:** bore an **elephant** (symbol of strength and wisdom).
## SUB 4 | Demand for a Constituent Assembly and the Cabinet Mission
[1:4:0] TABLE head: Year | Step
  [1:4:0.0] 1895 | Constitution of India Bill – first explicit demand for fundamental rights
  [1:4:0.1] 1922 | Gandhi: India's destiny to be decided by Indians
  [1:4:0.2] 1931 | Karachi Congress – Resolution on Fundamental Rights and Economic Policy
  [1:4:0.3] 1934 | M.N. Roy first put forward the idea; Swaraj Party – first formal demand (Ranchi, May 1934); Congress adopted it (Patna, May 1934; CWC resolution June 1934)
  [1:4:0.4] 1938 | Nehru – CA elected on adult franchise
  [1:4:0.5] 1940 | August Offer – British accepted the principle
  [1:4:0.6] 1942 | Cripps Mission – proposal for an elected Assembly
  [1:4:0.7] 1946 | Constituted under the Cabinet Mission Plan (16 May 1946)
[1:4:1] - **Set up under the Cabinet Mission Plan, 1946** – **not** the 1935 Act, Cripps, Wavell Plan, Simon Commission or the Independence Act.
[1:4:2] - **Cabinet Mission** (arrived **24 March 1946**): **Pethick-Lawrence** (Secretary of State for India), **Stafford Cripps** (Board of Trade), **A.V. Alexander** (Admiralty) – **NOT William Wood**.
[1:4:3] - **Cripps proposals (1942):** Assembly **elected** by the lower houses of provincial legislatures by PR (princely states to **send** representatives); **any province not accepting** the new Constitution could **sign a separate agreement** with Britain.
[1:4:4] > UPSC 2022: A ("members nominated by the Provincial Assemblies") ✗; B (province's right to a separate agreement) ✓ → **B only**.
[1:4:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.123-130; NextGen Indian History - APPSC 06 p.385-386; Rocket Polity #5 p.14, p.3; app notes 2:1 sub 0
## SUB 5 | Interim Government (1946) and the First Cabinet
[1:5:0] - **Announced 24 August 1946**; **took charge 2 September 1946** (from the newly elected Assembly, under the Cabinet Mission scheme); lasted till 15 August 1947.
[1:5:1] - **Viceroy** = ex-officio **President** of the Executive Council; **Nehru** = **Vice-President** – the de facto head of the Interim Cabinet.
[1:5:2] - **Muslim League** joined in **October 1946**.
[1:5:3] TABLE head: Member | Portfolio
  [1:5:3.0] Nehru | External Affairs and Commonwealth Relations
  [1:5:3.1] Sardar Patel | Home, Information and Broadcasting
  [1:5:3.2] Rajendra Prasad | Food and Agriculture
  [1:5:3.3] Baldev Singh | Defence
  [1:5:3.4] Liaquat Ali Khan (League) | Finance
  [1:5:3.5] Jagjivan Ram | Labour
  [1:5:3.6] C. Rajagopalachari | Education and Arts (at formation)
  [1:5:3.7] C.H. Bhabha | Works, Mines and Power
  [1:5:3.8] Jogendra Nath Mandal (League) | Law
[1:5:4] - **NOT a member:** **Dr S. Radhakrishnan** (nor Jinnah).
[1:5:5] - **First cabinet of free India (15 Aug 1947):** **B.R. Ambedkar – Law**; **Sardar Patel – first Deputy Prime Minister** (and Home); **first Finance Minister R.K. Shanmukham Chetty** (1947-48), then **John Mathai**.
[1:5:6] - **Nehru** took the oath as first PM on 15 Aug 1947 from Governor-General **Lord Mountbatten**.
[1:5:7] - **Direct Action Day (16 Aug 1946):** the Muslim League's call to press for **Pakistan** after it withdrew its acceptance of the Cabinet Mission Plan → **Calcutta riots**.
[1:5:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.130; NextGen Indian History - APPSC 06 p.386-389 (biographies); Rocket Polity p.3; app notes 2:1 sub 8
## SUB 6 | Time, Sessions and Size of the Text
[1:6:0] TABLE head: Item | Figure
  [1:6:0.0] Time taken | 2 years, 11 months, 18 days (9 Dec 1946 – 26 Nov 1949) – about 3 years (Rocket prints 17 days)
  [1:6:0.1] Sessions / sitting days | 11 sessions / 165 days; with the 24 Jan 1950 meeting, 12 sittings-sessions in all
  [1:6:0.2] Draft considered | 114 days
  [1:6:0.3] Amendments | 7,635 proposed, 2,473 discussed
  [1:6:0.4] B.N. Rau's draft (Oct 1947) | 243 Articles, 13 Schedules
  [1:6:0.5] Draft Constitution (Feb 1948) | 315 Articles
  [1:6:0.6] Original Constitution | Preamble, 395 Articles, 22 Parts (chapters), 8 Schedules
  [1:6:0.7] Today (2026) | about 470 Articles, 25 Parts, 12 Schedules
  [1:6:0.8] Cost | about Rs 64 lakh; constitutions of about 60 countries studied
  [1:6:0.9] Signed | 284 of 299 members, on 24 January 1950
[1:6:1] - **Copies signed on 24 Jan 1950:** **three** – one **hand-written in English**, one **printed in English**, one **hand-written in Hindi** (Prasad's announcement); about 1,000 photolithographic copies were later printed by the Survey of India, Dehradun.
[1:6:2] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.137, p.141-144, p.151-152; Rocket Polity #5 p.15; constitutionofindia.net (CAD 24 Jan 1950); app notes 2:1 sub 6, 11

## SUB 8 | Indian Independence Act, 1947 and the Transition
[1:8:0] # Mountbatten Plan and the Act
[1:8:1] - **3 June 1947 Plan (Mountbatten)** – partition, accepted by Congress and League – the **basis of the Indian Independence Act**.
[1:8:2] - Bill introduced in the Commons **4 July 1947**; passed / **royal assent 18 July 1947**.
[1:8:3] - **Radcliffe** chaired the **Punjab** and **Bengal** Boundary Commissions; award published **17 August 1947**.
[1:8:4] # Provisions
[1:8:5] - Ended **British sovereignty**; two independent **Dominions – India and Pakistan** – from **15 August 1947**.
[1:8:6] - **Governor-General** (common or separate) to act on **Dominion ministers'** advice; British monarch nominal head; **Crown lost its veto**.
[1:8:7] - **Constituent Assembly** = sovereign constitution-maker **and** Dominion legislature; the **Central Legislative Assembly ended**.
[1:8:8] - **Paramountcy lapsed**: about **565** princely states theoretically free; expected to join India or Pakistan.
[1:8:9] - **Instrument of Accession:** ceded **defence, external affairs, communications** (NOT finance); **Standstill Agreement** – Hyderabad, November 1947; **Kashmir** acceded **26 October 1947**; UN ceasefire 1 January 1949.
[1:8:10] - Dominion status ended with the Republic, **26 Jan 1950**.
[1:8:11] # Governors-General and after
[1:8:12] - **Mountbatten**: last Viceroy and **first GG of independent India**; **C. Rajagopalachari**: last and only Indian GG (June 1948 – Jan 1950); he assented to the **Madras Estates (Abolition) Act** on 2 April 1949.
[1:8:13] - **Nehru's "Tryst with Destiny"** ("At the stroke of the midnight hour, when the world sleeps, India will awake to life and freedom") – night of **14–15 August 1947**, in the Assembly.
[1:8:14] - After 26 Jan 1950 the Assembly worked as the **Provisional Parliament** till **17 April 1952**; the **first Lok Sabha** was constituted on 17 April 1952 and its **first session began 13 May 1952**.
[1:8:15] - Significance: sovereignty came with partition; Congress accepted dominion status for a quick, peaceful transfer.
[1:8:16] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC 06 p.386-389; NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.113, p.137; Rocket Polity #22 p.53; en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Lok_Sabha (Lok Sabha bulletin); app notes 2:1 sub 9
## SUB 9 | Speeches, Remarks and Criticisms
[1:9:0] TABLE head: Statement | Speaker
  [1:9:0.0] "The first task of this Assembly is to free India through a new Constitution, to feed the starving people and clothe the naked masses…" | Nehru (Objectives Resolution debate, Jan 1947)
  [1:9:0.1] "We must hold fast to constitutional methods of achieving our social and economic objectives"; civil disobedience = "grammar of anarchy"; "a life of contradictions" | Ambedkar, closing speech 25 Nov 1949
  [1:9:0.2] Constitutional morality "is not a natural sentiment. It has to be cultivated" (from George Grote) | Ambedkar, 4 Nov 1948
  [1:9:0.3] "A Constitution, like a machine, is a lifeless thing…" | Rajendra Prasad, 26 Nov 1949
  [1:9:0.4] "There is no permanence in Constitutions. There should be a certain flexibility" | Nehru, 8 Nov 1948 (also Brajeshwar Prasad); H.V. Kamath wanted safeguards against hasty amendment
  [1:9:0.5] "A one-party body in an essentially one-party country"; "the Assembly was the Congress and the Congress was India" | Granville Austin
  [1:9:0.6] "A body of Hindus" | Viscount Simon
  [1:9:0.7] Represented "only one major community" | Winston Churchill
  [1:9:0.8] "Drifting Committee" | Naziruddin Ahmed
[1:9:1] - **Criticisms:** **not representative** (no adult franchise); **not sovereign** (British creation); **time-consuming** (about 3 years vs about 4 months for the USA); **Congress-dominated**; **lawyer-politician** dominated; **"borrowed" / patchwork**.
[1:9:2] - **Defence:** multi-party body; worked through committees; invited public submissions.
[1:9:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.143; en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amendment_of_the_Constitution_of_India (CAD Vol. VII p.322-323); constitutionofindia.net (CAD 25 Nov 1949); app notes 2:1 sub 10
[1:9:4] - **"Constitution a sacred document":** Dr **B.R. Ambedkar** (a staunch constitutionalist) – it must be treated as basic and sacred.
## SUB 10 | Allied Firsts Asked with This Topic
[1:10:0] - **Kher Committee** (B.G. Kher, 1948-49) – on ways and means of achieving **universal elementary education within ten years at less cost** (the Constitution's ten-year goal); a Government of India / CABE committee.
[1:10:1] - **First Indian in the British Parliament (House of Commons):** **Dadabhai Naoroji** (1892, Liberal, Finsbury Central).
[1:10:2] - **Fundamental Rights resolution:** adopted at the **Karachi session of the INC (1931)**.
[1:10:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC 06 p.389 (Dadabhai Naoroji); csdindia.org (Govinda, UEE in India – Kher Committee); NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.123
## SUB 11 | Linguistic Provinces – Dar Commission and JVP Committee
[1:11:0] - **Linguistic Provinces Commission** (**17 June 1948**): **S.K. Dar**, retired Allahabad HC judge.
[1:11:1] - **Dar Commission** and **JVP Committee** (**J**awaharlal, **V**allabhbhai, **P**attabhi Sitaramayya) both **rejected linguistic states** for the time being; JVP left room for **Andhra**.
[1:11:2] - **AP angle:** the Madras Assembly resolved (four months before independence) to divide the Presidency into **Andhra, Tamil, Karnataka, Kerala**; Nehru accepted the Andhra demand **27 Nov 1947**; Andhra dropped from the Draft of 21 Feb 1948; the demand led to **Andhra State, 1953**.
[1:11:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 2:1 sub 4; NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (States reorganisation)

=== NOTES 2:2 | Sources of the Indian Constitution – Borrowed Features and Their Origin Countries
## SUB 0 | Ireland and South Africa – Directive Principles, President's Election, Rajya Sabha, Amendment
[2:0:0] # Ireland (Eire, Constitution of 1937)
[2:0:1] - **Directive Principles of State Policy (Part IV, Arts. 36-51)** – borrowed from the **Irish Constitution** (Ireland itself took the idea from **Spain**) (asked 11 times: APPSC G2 2003, MPDO 1998, TWO 2012, SI 2019; UPPSC, MPPSC 2023).
[2:0:2] - **Method of election of the President** (indirect, proportional representation by single transferable vote) – Ireland.
[2:0:3] - **Nomination of members to the Rajya Sabha** (12 by the President) – Ireland.
[2:0:4] - **⚠ Trap:** DPSP is **not** from Weimar Germany, South Africa, Canada or Britain; Ambedkar called the DPSP a **"novel feature"** of the Constitution.
[2:0:5] # South Africa
[2:0:6] - **Procedure for amendment of the Constitution (Art. 368)** – South Africa (SSC 2016; APPSC FSO).
[2:0:7] - **Election of members of the Rajya Sabha** (by elected MLAs, proportional representation) – South Africa (APPSC 2019).
[2:0:8] - **⚠ Trap:** the amendment procedure is **not** from France, Ireland, Britain or the USA (APPSC Lecturers 2018: "Amendment – France" is the wrong statement).
[2:0:9] > RS pair: **election** of RS members = **South Africa**; **nomination** to the RS and the **President's election** = **Ireland**.
[2:0:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.154-155 (sources table); Rocket Polity #1 p.3; app notes 2:2 sub 5
## SUB 1 | USA – Fundamental Rights, Judicial Review, Preamble and the Judiciary
[2:1:0] TABLE head: Feature | Borrowed from
  [2:1:0.0] Fundamental Rights (Part III, Arts. 12-35) | USA (Bill of Rights)
  [2:1:0.1] Judicial review | USA
  [2:1:0.2] Independence of the judiciary | USA
  [2:1:0.3] Idea of the Preamble | USA
  [2:1:0.4] Post of Vice-President (ex officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha) | USA
  [2:1:0.5] Impeachment of the President | USA
  [2:1:0.6] Removal of Supreme Court and High Court judges | USA
  [2:1:0.7] Written Constitution | USA – the world's first written Constitution (1787), also the shortest (7 Articles)
[2:1:1] - **Fundamental Rights = USA** (asked 6 times: APPSC Degree Lecturers 2020; UPPSC, UPRO 2014, UDA 2010).
[2:1:2] - **Judicial review originated in the USA** – *Marbury v. Madison* (1803); the words "judicial review" appear in **neither** the US nor the Indian text; **Britain** (parliamentary sovereignty, no written constitution) has **no judicial review** of Acts of Parliament.
[2:1:3] - **Preamble idea = USA** (asked 5 times); only the **language** of the Preamble is from **Australia**; liberty, equality, fraternity = France.
[2:1:4] - Judicial review in India is narrower: Art. 21 uses **"procedure established by law"** (Japan), not the US **"due process of law"**.
[2:1:5] - **USA = presidential** form of government (India, Britain, Canada are parliamentary).
[2:1:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.154-155, p.164; Rocket Polity #75 (Rule of Law, Part 2 p.85-86); app notes 2:2 sub 3
## SUB 2 | Britain – Parliamentary Features, Rule of Law, Single Citizenship
[2:2:0] TABLE head: Feature | Borrowed from
  [2:2:0.0] Parliamentary government, cabinet system | Britain
  [2:2:0.1] Single citizenship (USA has dual citizenship) | Britain
  [2:2:0.2] Bicameralism | Britain
  [2:2:0.3] Rule of Law, legislative (law-making) procedure | Britain
  [2:2:0.4] Office of Speaker and his role | Britain
  [2:2:0.5] Prerogative writs ("petitions") | Britain
  [2:2:0.6] Parliamentary privileges | Britain
  [2:2:0.7] Nominal head (President, like the Crown) | Britain
[2:2:1] - "Which feature is from Britain?" – **cabinet system**, **single citizenship**, **bicameralism**, **writs** (APPSC 2019, FRO; SSC 2019). **Independence of judiciary** is US, not British (APPSC Panchayat Secy 2017 → **1, 2, 3**).
[2:2:2] - **Britain has no single written constitution**, but the **concept of constitutional government** goes back to the **Magna Carta (1215)**; the first **written** constitution is the US one.
[2:2:3] - **Rule of Law** – A.V. **Dicey** (1885): supremacy of law, equality before law, and rights of the individual secured by courts; its main objective is to secure the **liberty of citizens** against arbitrary power. A **basic feature** (Kesavananda).
[2:2:4] - **Single citizenship** – one of the framers' crucial steps to **tackle regionalism** and build national unity.
[2:2:5] - **Contempt of court:** the power of courts to punish for contempt comes from **English common law** (British-era courts; now Arts. 129, 215).
[2:2:6] - **Integrated judiciary:** one hierarchy (SC → HCs → subordinate courts) enforces both Central and State laws, unlike the US dual courts; this **single system of courts was adopted from the GoI Act 1935** (Laxmikanth), so "borrowed from the British system" is the false reason; India's judicial system follows the British common-law pattern.
[2:2:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.154-155, p.164-165, p.187-188; Rocket Polity #75 (Part 2 p.85-87); Laxmikanth, Indian Polity ch. Salient Features (integrated judiciary); en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contempt_of_court_in_India; app notes 2:2 sub 2
## SUB 3 | Federal Features – Canada and Australia
[2:3:0] # Canada
[2:3:1] - **Federation with a strong Centre** – Indian federalism follows the **Canadian** pattern (SSC 2008; UPPSC 2017; APPSC DAO 2011).
[2:3:2] - **"Union of States" (Art. 1):** **not the result of an agreement** among States and **no right to secede**; the Republic of India rests on the **declared will of the people** ("We, the people of India").
[2:3:3] - **Residuary powers with the Centre** – **Art. 248** (Parliament) – Canada (SSC CGL 2019; APPSC HWO); in the **USA and Australia** residuary powers lie with the **States**.
[2:3:4] - **Appointment of State Governors by the Centre** – Canada.
[2:3:5] - **Advisory jurisdiction of the Supreme Court (Art. 143)** – Canada.
[2:3:6] # Australia
[2:3:7] - **Concurrent List** – Australia (asked 7 times: APPSC 2017, Social History 2019, G2; UPPSC 2015, 2016).
[2:3:8] - **Freedom of trade, commerce and intercourse** – Australia (not Canada).
[2:3:9] - **Joint sitting** of the two Houses (**Art. 108**, presided over by the **Lok Sabha Speaker**) – Australia.
[2:3:10] - **Language of the Preamble** – Australia.
[2:3:11] - Makers followed the Canadian scheme of division but **added a third list** (Concurrent); in a clash, the **Union law prevails**.
[2:3:12] - **Seventh Schedule today:** Union **98**, State **59**, Concurrent **52** (originally **97, 66, 47**).
[2:3:13] > Residuary powers = **Canada**, not USA; Concurrent List = **Australia**, not Canada. With no 1935 option, "scheme of federation" = **Canada**.
[2:3:14] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.154-155; Rocket Polity #1 p.3; app notes 2:2 sub 4, sub 7
## SUB 4 | Government of India Act 1935 – the Largest Source, and the Length of the Constitution
[2:4:0] - **Single largest source:** federal scheme, **office of Governor**, **judiciary**, **Public Service Commissions**, **emergency provisions**, administrative details – "larger part" of the Constitution (APPSC JA 2012).
[2:4:1] - **Greatest / most profound influence** on the Constitution = **GoI Act 1935** (about 250 provisions), not the British Constitution (APPSC G1 2002; G1 PYQ – some answer keys gave "British Constitution").
[2:4:2] - **Federal system and distribution of powers** between Centre and States = the **1935 Act** scheme (UPSC 2012; APPSC G2).
[2:4:3] - **1935 lists:** Federal (**59**), Provincial (**54**), **Concurrent (36)** items (SI 2023) → today's **Union, State and Concurrent Lists**.
[2:4:4] - **Not** a 1935 constituent: a **written Constitution made by Indians** – accepted in principle only by the **August Offer, 1940** (UPPSC 2010).
[2:4:5] # Why the Constitution is the lengthiest
[2:4:6] - **Factors:** **vast geography and diversity**; **bulky 1935 Act**; a **single Constitution for Centre and States**; dominance of **legal luminaries**; detailed administrative and **social-economic** provisions; framers' wish to **safeguard the young democracy**.
[2:4:7] - **NOT a factor:** "adoption of the American model" (APPSC ATWO); **local bodies** were **not** in the original Constitution (Parts IX, IXA added in **1992**) (APPSC ASWO Mains).
[2:4:8] - **Original:** **395 Articles, 22 Parts, 8 Schedules** → now about **470 Articles, 25 Parts, 12 Schedules** (as of 2026).
[2:4:9] - **Criticisms:** **"bag of borrowings"** (sources), **"elephantine"** (length), **"lawyers' paradise"** (legal detail); reply – borrowed features were **adapted** to Indian conditions.
[2:4:10] - **Philosophical part** (FRs, DPSPs) from the USA and Ireland; **political part** (parliamentary system) from Britain; **structural part** from the 1935 Act.
[2:4:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.154-155, p.160-161; Indian_Polity Part 1 (lengthiest constitution); app notes 2:2 sub 0-1; rebuilt notes 2:0 (GoI Act 1935)
## SUB 5 | Emergency, USSR, France, Japan
[2:5:0] TABLE head: Feature | Borrowed from
  [2:5:0.0] Emergency provisions (general scheme) | GoI Act 1935; with no 1935 option → Germany (Weimar)
  [2:5:0.1] Suspension of Fundamental Rights during Emergency | Weimar Constitution of Germany
  [2:5:0.2] Fundamental Duties (Art. 51A) | USSR (added by the 42nd Amendment, 1976; 11th duty by the 86th, 2002)
  [2:5:0.3] Ideals of justice – social, economic, political (Preamble) | USSR
  [2:5:0.4] Five-Year Plans / Planning Commission idea | USSR
  [2:5:0.5] Republic; liberty, equality, fraternity (Preamble) | France
  [2:5:0.6] Procedure established by law (Art. 21) | Japan
[2:5:1] - **Emergency:** "on the lines of" Germany (APPSC 2017; NDA 2009 – Weimar); **not** from South Africa, the USA or the UK.
[2:5:2] - In a National Emergency FRs can be suspended **except Arts. 20 and 21**; **Art. 19** is the most easily suspended.
[2:5:3] - **⚠ Trap:** "Ideals of **Justice**" is the one Preamble ideal **not** from France – it is from the USSR (APPSC DAO 2020); Fundamental Duties = USSR, not France.
[2:5:4] - **Election Commission (Art. 324)** – an Indian provision, not inspired by any foreign constitution (JPSC 2023).
[2:5:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.154-155; Rocket Polity #1 p.3; app notes 2:2 sub 6

[2:6:1] - **Industrial Revolution:** first in **Britain** (18th century).
[2:6:2] - **Foreign investment in India in 1948:** largest share held by **Great Britain**.
[2:6:3] - **US Vice-President:** **Mike Pence** (2017-21) when asked; **now JD Vance** (since 20 Jan 2025).
[2:6:4] - **Women's Hockey World Cup:** **Netherlands** – 9 titles (8th in 2018, 9th in 2022); **Argentina won the 2026 World Cup** (Amstelveen, 29 Aug 2026; its 3rd title).
[2:6:5] - **Miss Universe 2019:** **Zozibini Tunzi** of **South Africa**; Miss Universe 2025: **Fátima Bosch** (Mexico).
[2:6:6] - **Disposable vapes:** **France** announced a ban plan in **September 2023** (national anti-smoking plan).

## SUB 7 | Country → Features Master List and Match Questions
[2:7:0] TABLE head: Source | Features
  [2:7:0.0] GoI Act 1935 | Federal scheme, Governor, judiciary, PSCs, Emergency, administrative details
  [2:7:0.1] Britain | Parliamentary govt, cabinet, single citizenship, bicameralism, Rule of Law, legislative procedure, Speaker, writs, privileges
  [2:7:0.2] USA | FRs, judicial review, independent judiciary, Preamble idea, Vice-President, impeachment, removal of judges
  [2:7:0.3] Ireland | DPSP, President's election, nomination to RS
  [2:7:0.4] Canada | Strong-Centre federation, residuary powers, Governors by Centre, advisory jurisdiction
  [2:7:0.5] Australia | Concurrent List, freedom of trade, joint sitting, Preamble language
  [2:7:0.6] Weimar Germany | Suspension of FRs in Emergency
  [2:7:0.7] USSR | Fundamental Duties, justice ideals, Five-Year Plans
  [2:7:0.8] France | Republic; liberty, equality, fraternity
  [2:7:0.9] South Africa | Amendment procedure, election of RS members
  [2:7:0.10] Japan | Procedure established by law
[2:7:1] > UPSC 2003: DPSP – Ireland, FRs – USA, Concurrent List – Australia, Union of States with a strong Union – Canada → **A-3, B-5, C-1, D-2**.
[2:7:2] > APPSC G2 Mains 2025: Emergency – **GoI Act 1935**, suspension of FRs – **Germany**, Concurrent List – **Australia**, single citizenship – **UK** → **a-iii, b-iv, c-ii, d-i**.
[2:7:3] > APPSC ATWO: countries drawn on – **USA, France, Ireland, South Africa** (1, 2, 4, 5); a "written British Constitution" does not exist.
