[nbff32f90f3c] sub7
  Which of the following sets consists entirely of features borrowed from the Constitution of the United States of America?
  Judicial review, parliamentary system, independence of judiciary and single citizenship | *Fundamental Rights, judicial review, independence of judiciary and impeachment of the President | Fundamental Rights, procedure established by law, joint sitting and impeachment of the President | Impeachment of the President, Directive Principles of State Policy, removal of judges and joint sitting
  x: Fundamental Rights, judicial review, independence of the judiciary and impeachment of the President are among the features borrowed from the United States. Procedure established by law is associated with Japan, while joint sitting is associated with Australia.
[nfb5eeddb9b3] sub7
  Which one of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
  Procedure established by law — Japan | Suspension of Fundamental Rights during Emergency — Weimar Germany | Republic and liberty, equality and fraternity — France | *Fundamental Duties — France
  x: Fundamental Duties were borrowed from the USSR, not France. Weimar Germany, France and Japan are correctly associated with the features given in the other options.
[nindian-polity-3-2] sub7
  Which of the following statements is incorrect?
  The procedure established by law was borrowed from Japan. | The idea of a Republic was borrowed from France. | *The procedure for constitutional amendment was borrowed from Ireland. | Emergency provisions, except financial emergency, were borrowed from Germany.
  x: C is incorrect: the amendment procedure was borrowed from South Africa, not Ireland (Ireland gave DPSP). Procedure established by law is from Japan, the Republic from France, and Emergency provisions (except financial) from Germany's Weimar Constitution.
[nindian-polity-3-7] sub7
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  Impeachment of the President – Ireland | *Single citizenship – Britain | Method of election of the President – USA | Joint sitting of both Houses – Britain
  x: Single citizenship came from Britain; impeachment of the President is from the USA, the method of electing the President from Ireland and the joint sitting from Australia.
[f03315ee819d5] sub7
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  *Suspension of Fundamental Rights in Emergency – Weimar Germany | Advisory jurisdiction of the Supreme Court – Australia | Freedom of trade and commerce – Canada | Procedure established by law – USA
  x: Suspension of FRs is from Weimar Germany; advisory jurisdiction is from Canada, freedom of trade from Australia and procedure established by law from Japan.
[g3b56d24b1519] sub7
  Match the following:
List-I (Borrowed feature)
a. Directive Principles of State Policy
b. Procedure for amendment of the Constitution
c. Residuary powers with the Centre
d. Procedure established by law
List-II (Source)
i. Canada
ii. Japan
iii. Ireland
iv. South Africa
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
  *a-iii, b-iv, c-i, d-ii | a-iv, b-iii, c-i, d-ii | a-iii, b-iv, c-ii, d-i | a-i, b-iv, c-iii, d-ii
  x: DPSP came from Ireland, amendment procedure from South Africa, residuary powers with the Centre from Canada and 'procedure established by law' from Japan.

=== NOTES 2:3 | Salient Features of the Indian Constitution
## SUB 0 | Democracy, Republic and Parliamentary Government
[3:0:0] # Democracy
[3:0:1] - **Word origin:** **Greek** – *demos* (people) + *kratos* (rule); not Latin, Roman or French (APPSC G2 Mains 2019; Town Planning).
[3:0:2] - **India = representative (indirect) democracy:** people elect representatives who govern (APPSC DL 2017; SSC CGL 2016).
[3:0:3] - **Direct democracy is NOT found in India** at the national level – no **referendum, initiative or recall** (APSLPRB 2019: "Direct democracy is found in India" = the incorrect statement).
[3:0:4] - **Tools of direct democracy:** **recall** = removing an elected representative before his term; **referendum** = people vote on a law/issue; **initiative** = people propose a law; **plebiscite** = people's verdict on a political question (APSLPRB Constable 2023: recall).
[3:0:5] - **Democracy needs people's participation:** without participation and co-operation democracy fails → **people should participate and co-operate with the government** (not compulsion, not a switch to the presidential system).
[3:0:6] - India is a **democratic country** and also has a **Constitution of its own** – both true, but having a constitution does not by itself make a country democratic (UPPSC 2017 A–R: both true, R not the explanation).
[3:0:7] - **Not authoritarian:** India has democratic government, rule of law and a government that follows the Constitution; **authoritarian government is NOT a feature** (UPPSC Mains 2011).
[3:0:8] # Republic
[3:0:9] - **Republic = no hereditary ruler; the head of State is elected** (directly or indirectly) for a fixed term (UPPSC 1997, 2017; Panchayat Secretary).
[3:0:10] - India's head of State, the **President, is elected indirectly** – the parliamentary system is **combined with republicanism** (a nominal elected head, real power with ministers) (RAS 2013 A–R: A true, R true and the correct explanation).
[3:0:11] - **"Republic" in the Preamble** – idea from the **French** Constitution (with liberty, equality, fraternity).
[3:0:12] - India is a **"Union of States"** (Art. 1), a **Sovereign Socialist Secular Democratic Republic with a parliamentary system**.
[3:0:13] - **Cardinal features of the Indian political system:** **democratic republic**, **parliamentary form of government**, **supreme power vests in the people**, and a **unified authority** (one Constitution, single citizenship, integrated judiciary) → **all four** (UPPSC 2009).
[3:0:14] # Parliamentary form of government
[3:0:15] - India chose the **British parliamentary system**, not the American **presidential** system → India is a **parliamentary democracy** (UPPSC 2005); **presidential government is NOT a feature** (UPPSC Mains 2015).
[3:0:16] - **Parliamentary system** = **co-operation and co-ordination** between legislature and executive; **presidential** = **separation of powers**.
[3:0:17] - **Features:** nominal (President) and real (Council of Ministers) executive; majority party rule; **collective responsibility of the Council of Ministers to the Lok Sabha** (Art. 75); ministers are **members of the legislature**; leadership of PM/CM; dissolution of the lower House. Adopted at both **Centre and States**.
[3:0:18] - **Why chosen:** already **familiar** (worked under the GoI Acts of 1919 and 1935); suits India's **vast size and diversity**; avoids **executive–legislature conflict** seen in the USA.
[3:0:19] - **Origins:** parliamentary form began in **Britain**; presidential form in the **USA**.
[3:0:20] # India vs the British model (UPSC 2021)
[3:0:21] TABLE head: Point | India | Britain
  [3:0:21.0] Parliament | Not sovereign – a creature of a written Constitution; its law-making power is limited (FRs, federalism, judicial review) | Sovereign – supreme in legislation
  [3:0:21.1] Validity of laws / amendments | Courts can strike them down; cases on interpreting the Constitution (incl. validity of an Amendment) go to a Constitution Bench of at least 5 judges (Art. 145(3)) | No judicial review of Acts of Parliament
  [3:0:21.2] Head of State | Elected (indirectly) – republic | Hereditary monarch
  [3:0:21.3] Citizenship | Single | Single
[3:0:22] > UPSC 2021: both statements correct → **Both 1 and 2** (UPSC official key C).
[3:0:23] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.159-164 (parliamentary system), p.149, 155 (Preamble, French Republic), p.324-325 (judicial review); Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.2-3 p.26-43; mrunal.org/2022/05/upsc-prelims-2021-answerkey-errors.html (UPSC official key); app notes 2:3 sub 3-4
## SUB 1 | Written and Lengthiest Constitution – Parts, Schedules, Articles
[3:1:0] # Written and lengthiest
[3:1:1] - **A Constitution** = the document that lays down the **basic rules** for the people and the government (SSC 2018).
[3:1:2] - **Written constitution tradition began in the USA** – the **US Constitution (1787)** is the **first written** national constitution (and the shortest: 7 Articles); the **British** Constitution is **unwritten** (MPPSC 2015).
[3:1:3] - India's is the **lengthiest written constitution** of the world – longer than those of Canada, South Africa or Australia (APPSC HWO).
[3:1:4] - **Reasons for length:** vast size and **diversity**; influence of the bulky **GoI Act 1935**; **single Constitution for Centre and States**; dominance of **legal luminaries**; detailed **administrative provisions**; matters left to ordinary law elsewhere put in the Constitution.
[3:1:5] - **Original copies:** **two handwritten (calligraphed) originals – English and Hindi** – kept in helium/inert-gas cases in the **Parliament Library** (APPSC Inspector of Factories); English calligraphy by **Prem Behari Narain Raizada**, Hindi by **Vasant Krishan Vaidya**, decorated by Nandalal Bose's Santiniketan artists (members also signed a printed English version on 24 Jan 1950).
[3:1:6] # Number game
[3:1:7] TABLE head: Item | Original (26 Nov 1949) | Now (2026)
  [3:1:7.0] Articles | 395 (with Preamble) | about 470 (numbering still ends at Art. 395)
  [3:1:7.1] Parts | 22 | 25
  [3:1:7.2] Schedules | 8 | 12
[3:1:8] - **Schedules now = 12** (the 12th added by the 74th Amendment, 1992) – also 12 in 2003 (APPSC G2 2003; Junior Steno).
[3:1:9] - **Parts now = 25:** 22 + 4 added (**IVA, IXA, IXB, XIVA**) − 1 repealed (**VII**) (SSC 2017). Older books say 24 Parts – that count is from before Part IXB (97th Amendment, 2011).
[3:1:10] - New provisions are added as **lettered Articles** (e.g. **21A, 51A**); numbering still ends at **Article 395**.
[3:1:11] - **⚠ Trap:** some books print "9 original Schedules / 397 Articles" – wrong. Original = **8 Schedules, 395 Articles, 22 Parts**.
[3:1:12] # Parts added and repealed
[3:1:13] TABLE head: Part | Subject | Amendment
  [3:1:13.0] IVA | Fundamental Duties | 42nd, 1976
  [3:1:13.1] IX | Panchayats | 73rd, 1992 (the old Part IX on territories was repealed in 1956 and reused)
  [3:1:13.2] IXA | Municipalities | 74th, 1992
  [3:1:13.3] IXB | Co-operative Societies | 97th, 2011
  [3:1:13.4] XIVA | Tribunals | 42nd, 1976
  [3:1:13.5] VII | Repealed (Part B States) | 7th, 1956
[3:1:14] # Order of key Parts
[3:1:15] TABLE head: Part | Subject
  [3:1:15.0] I | The Union and its Territory (Art. 1: "India, that is Bharat, shall be a Union of States")
  [3:1:15.1] II | Citizenship (Arts. 5-11)
  [3:1:15.2] III | Fundamental Rights (e.g. abolition of titles, Art. 18)
  [3:1:15.3] IV | DPSP (e.g. organisation of village panchayats, Art. 40)
  [3:1:15.4] IVA | Fundamental Duties (e.g. parent's duty to give education to a child of 6-14, Art. 51A(k))
  [3:1:15.5] V | The Union (Union Executive, Parliament)
  [3:1:15.6] VIII | Union Territories
  [3:1:15.7] IX / IXA / IXB | Panchayats / Municipalities / Co-operative Societies
  [3:1:15.8] XVIII | Emergency Provisions
  [3:1:15.9] XX | Amendment of the Constitution (Art. 368)
  [3:1:15.10] XXII | Short title, commencement (Art. 394), authoritative Hindi text, repeals (Art. 395)
[3:1:16] - **Article 395 = Repeals:** it repealed the **Indian Independence Act, 1947** and the **Government of India Act, 1935** (not the commencement – that is Art. 394) (APPSC Asst. Social Welfare).
[3:1:17] > Match (CGPSC 2019): titles → FR; village panchayats → DPSP; education 6-14 duty → FD; "Union of States" → Union and its Territories → **3, 1, 4, 2**.
[3:1:18] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.141-142, p.154-160 (schedules, Parts table, lengthiest); Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.1 p.20-24 (calligraphy, signed copies), ch.2 p.26-30; byjus.com/ias-questions/how-many-handwritten-copies-of-the-indian-constitution-are-there; app notes 2:3 sub 1-2
[3:1:19] - **Living document:** a Constitution is a **living organism of functional institutions** – it grows with the people's aspirations (amendments, interpretation), not a static text of rules.
## SUB 2 | Constitutionalism and Supremacy of the Constitution
[3:2:0] # Constitutionalism
[3:2:1] - **Constitutionalism** = government whose powers are **limited** by a supreme Constitution; it **recognises the need to limit the powers of government** (APPSC G2 2011).
[3:2:2] - It is **not inherent in every constitution** – a country can have a constitution without constitutionalism (a dictator's constitution).
[3:2:3] - **Antithesis of constitutionalism = despotism** (absolute, unlimited rule); limited power and restraint of power are its features (APPSC G2 2011).
[3:2:4] - **Constitutional government** = one that places **effective restrictions on the authority of the State in the interest of individual liberty** – not restrictions on liberty in the interest of the State (UPSC 2014: **II only**).
[3:2:5] - **Limits in India:** Fundamental Rights (**Art. 13** – laws inconsistent with FRs are void), judicial review, federal division of powers, **basic structure** (**Kesavananda Bharati, 1973**).
[3:2:6] - Parliament's amending power is limited by the **basic structure doctrine**; basic-structure elements include **supremacy of the Constitution**, rule of law, separation of powers, judicial review, limited amending power.
[3:2:7] # Supremacy of the Constitution
[3:2:8] - The **Constitution is supreme** in the Indian political system – not Parliament, not the Supreme Court, not religion (UPPSC Mains 2013).
[3:2:9] - **Governmental organs owe their origin to the Constitution** – the Union and States derive their powers from it (APPSC Asst. Social Welfare).
[3:2:10] - **Why the Constitution is above Parliament:** the **Constitution specifies how Parliament is formed and what its powers are** (not because it is older or its makers were more eminent) (APPSC HWO).
[3:2:11] # Judicial review
[3:2:12] - **Judicial review** = power of the SC/HCs to declare laws and executive orders **unconstitutional**; the phrase is **nowhere used in the Constitution**, but **Arts. 13, 32, 226** (and others) **explicitly confer** the power.
[3:2:13] - **Basic structure is NOT defined in the Constitution** – it was evolved by the Supreme Court (Kesavananda Bharati, 1973) (UPSC 2020, statement I wrong).
[3:2:14] - **Synthesis** of British **parliamentary sovereignty** and American **judicial supremacy**: courts can strike down laws; Parliament can amend most of the Constitution.
[3:2:15] - **Scope narrower than in the USA:** India has **"procedure established by law"** (Art. 21), the USA **"due process of law"**; courts test a law only **against the supreme written Constitution** (FR violation, lack of competence, repugnancy), not its wisdom → judicial review is **restricted because the Constitution is supreme**, not because the executive or legislature is supreme.
[3:2:16] > UPSC 2020: II (Constitution provides for judicial review) is correct on the text of Arts. 13, 32, 226 → **Only II**. (UPSC's official key said D, "Neither", on the view that the Constitution never uses the term "judicial review".)
[3:2:17] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.164 (synthesis), p.324-325 (judicial review, phrase not used, narrower than USA); Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.1 p.3-4 (constitutionalism, K.C. Wheare), ch.2; mrunal.org/2021/11/upsc-prelims-2020-answerkey-error-discrepancy.html (official key D); app notes 2:3 sub 4
[3:2:18] - **Nature:** the Constitution is the **fundamental (foundation) law** of the land – every other law derives its authority from it (not common, derived or divine law).
[3:2:19] - **What a constitution cannot do:** it sets basic rules, limits power, protects rights and expresses shared values – it **cannot ensure that the 'right' people come to power**.
## SUB 3 | Other Salient Features – Amendment, Emergency, Rights, Duties, Franchise
[3:3:0] # Blend of rigidity and flexibility
[3:3:1] TABLE head: Method | Examples
  [3:3:1.0] Simple majority of Parliament (outside Art. 368) | Admission of new States, change of State boundaries
  [3:3:1.1] Special majority of Parliament (Art. 368) | Most provisions (e.g. Fundamental Rights)
  [3:3:1.2] Special majority + ratification by half the State legislatures | Federal provisions
[3:3:2] - **Amending power: Article 368** (Part XX) (APPSC Social History 2019).
[3:3:3] - **First amendment: 1951** (1st Amendment Act – added the 9th Schedule).
[3:3:4] - Neither as rigid as the US nor as flexible as the British Constitution.
[3:3:5] # Rights, duties, franchise
[3:3:6] - **Fundamental Rights:** six categories (Part III); all suspendable in a National Emergency except **Arts. 20 and 21**; Right to Property moved out → now **Art. 300A**. Rights for individuals make it a **liberal** constitution (NDA 2010).
[3:3:7] - **DPSP** (Part IV) – Ambedkar's "novel feature".
[3:3:8] - **Fundamental Duties:** added by the **42nd Amendment, 1976** on the **Swaran Singh Committee**'s recommendation – **10** duties; the **86th (2002)** added the 11th → **11** now (Part IVA, Art. 51A).
[3:3:9] - **Universal adult franchise** from the start (1950) at **21 years**; voting age **changed once – 21 → 18 by the 61st Amendment (1988, effective 1989)**; the same age for Lok Sabha and Assembly elections; no literacy or property test.
[3:3:10] - **Education:** moved from the State List to the **Concurrent List** by the **42nd Amendment, 1976**.
[3:3:11] # Emergency, bodies, tiers
[3:3:12] - **Three kinds of emergency:** **National (Art. 352)**, **State / President's Rule (Art. 356)**, **Financial (Art. 360)** – Part XVIII, Arts. 352-360 (APSLPRB SI 2017).
[3:3:13] - **Independent bodies:** ECI, CAG, UPSC, SPSCs.
[3:3:14] - **Integrated and independent judiciary:** one court system enforces **both Central and State laws**.
[3:3:15] - **Three-tier government:** **73rd and 74th Amendments (1992)** added the local tier.
[3:3:16] - **Kerala** was formed on **1 November 1956** (States Reorganisation Act, 1956) in place of the **Part B State of Travancore-Cochin** (APPSC MPDO 1998).
[3:3:17] # What the original Constitution (1950) did NOT contain
[3:3:18] TABLE head: Item | Added by
  [3:3:18.0] "Secular" (Preamble) | 42nd Amendment, 1976
  [3:3:18.1] Fundamental Duties | 42nd Amendment, 1976
  [3:3:18.2] "Basic structure" | Never written in – Supreme Court, Kesavananda Bharati 1973
  [3:3:18.3] UPSC | Original (Art. 315)
[3:3:19] > APPSC JA (NTR Univ.): of basic structure, secular, FDs and UPSC, only **UPSC (D only)** was in the original Constitution.
[3:3:20] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.157 (9th Schedule, 1st Amendment 1951), p.161 (rigidity), p.165-167 (FDs, Swaran Singh, franchise, independent bodies), p.337, 349 (61st Amendment); Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.2 p.30-35 (three emergencies); app notes 2:3 sub 6, 8
## SUB 4 | Who Said What – Opinions on the Constitution
[3:4:0] TABLE head: Description | Person
  [3:4:0.0] "Quasi-federal" – a unitary state with subsidiary federal features | K.C. Wheare
  [3:4:0.1] "Cooperative federalism" / cooperative federation | Granville Austin
  [3:4:0.2] Constitution is "first and foremost a social document" | Granville Austin
  [3:4:0.3] Federation with a strong centralising tendency | Ivor Jennings
  [3:4:0.4] Bargaining federalism | Morris-Jones
  [3:4:0.5] Can be unitary or federal as time and circumstances require | B.R. Ambedkar
  [3:4:0.6] "Elephantine size" – the bulkiest constitution, like the elephant on the Assembly's seal | H.V. Kamath
  [3:4:0.7] Assembly = "a one-party body in an essentially one-party country" | Granville Austin
  [3:4:0.8] Assembly = "a body of Hindus" | Viscount Simon
[3:4:1] - **Granville Austin's book:** ***The Indian Constitution: Cornerstone of a Nation*** (1966) (APPSC G2 2008).
[3:4:2] - **Other books in that PYQ:** *Introduction to the Study of the Law of the Constitution* = **A.V. Dicey**; *Modern Constitutions* = **K.C. Wheare** (1951).
[3:4:3] - **Criticisms:** "borrowed constitution", "**carbon copy of the 1935 Act**", "un-Indian", "elephantine", "**lawyers' paradise**".
[3:4:4] - **⚠ Trap:** elephantine = **Kamath** (not Ambedkar, Rajendra Prasad or Munshi); quasi-federal = **Wheare**; cooperative federalism = **Austin** (not Jennings or Chagla).
[3:4:5] # Borrowed character
[3:4:6] TABLE head: Feature | Model
  [3:4:6.0] Written constitution | USA
  [3:4:6.1] Parliamentary system | Britain
  [3:4:6.2] Strong Centre | Canada
  [3:4:6.3] Concurrent List | Australia
  [3:4:6.4] Amendment procedure | South Africa
[3:4:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.155 (borrowed features), p.163 (variously described), PYQ Q.147 p.289 (quasi-federal = Wheare); Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.2 p.35 (Wheare, Ambedkar, cooperative federalism); app notes 2:3 sub 0
[3:4:8] - **D.D. Basu:** *Introduction to the Constitution of India* (also the multi-volume *Commentary on the Constitution of India*).
## SUB 5 | Federal System with Unitary Bias
[3:5:0] TABLE head: Federal features | Unitary features
  [3:5:0.0] Two levels of government (later three) | Strong Centre (Canada model)
  [3:5:0.1] Division of powers (three Lists, 7th Schedule) | Single citizenship
  [3:5:0.2] Written, supreme Constitution | Integrated judiciary
  [3:5:0.3] Rigid constitution | Emergency provisions
  [3:5:0.4] Independent judiciary | States have no separate constitutions
  [3:5:0.5] Bicameral Parliament | Governor appointed by the Centre; all-India services
[3:5:1] - The word **"federation" is not used** in the Constitution; India is a "Union of States".
[3:5:2] - **Prominent federal features** asked: **division of powers**, **independent judiciary**, **supremacy of the Constitution** – "**allocation of financial resources**" is **not** listed as one (APPSC G2 Prelims 2017); the Finance Commission, integrated judiciary and emergency powers are not "the" federal feature (Sachivalayam: **division of powers**).
[3:5:3] - **Single citizenship**, not dual: **dual citizenship is NOT a feature** (APPSC Draughtsman); Part II deals with citizenship.
[3:5:4] - **Quasi-federal**: federal in form, unitary in spirit (Wheare); in an emergency the structure turns **unitary without a formal amendment**.
[3:5:5] - Indian States have **no separate constitutions and no dual citizenship** (the USA has both).
[3:5:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.160, 162-163 (federal / unitary features), p.167 (single citizenship); Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.2 p.34-35; app notes 2:3 sub 5, 9
## SUB 6 | Secular State
[3:6:0] - India is a **secular state**: it **does not uphold any religion as the official religion** – the Indian State has **no religious basis** (APPSC Asst. Director).
[3:6:1] - **"Secular" was added to the Preamble** by the **42nd Amendment, 1976** → the **Preamble** describes India as a secular State (Deputy Information Engineers).
[3:6:2] - **Indian model (positive):** equal respect to / equal protection of all religions; **Western model (negative):** complete separation of State and religion.
[3:6:3] - **Secular provisions:** Preamble; Arts. 14, 15, 16, **25-28** (freedom of religion), 29, 30, 44.
[3:6:4] - **Religious (theocratic) state is NOT a feature** of the Constitution (APPSC DL 2020).
[3:6:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (full text) p.166-167 (secular state, provisions, positive concept); app notes 2:3 sub 7
[3:6:6] - **NOT a component of secularism:** majority sentiment on **cow protection** – Art. 48 is a DPSP on organising agriculture and animal husbandry, not a secular provision.
## SUB 7 | Stray PYQs Filed Here (Other Subjects)
[3:7:0] - **Flag Code of India, 2002:** divided into **three Parts** – I general description of the flag; II display by public, private bodies and educational institutions; III display by Central/State governments and their agencies.
[3:7:1] - **Gagné's hierarchy of learning:** **eight** types – signal, stimulus–response, chaining, verbal association, multiple discrimination, concept, principle (rule), problem solving.
[3:7:2] - **Agnosia** = impaired ability to **recognise familiar objects** (with intact senses).
[3:7:3] - **Animal phyla:** Ctenophora – **bioluminescence**; Porifera – **choanocytes** (collar cells); Coelenterata (Cnidaria) – **polyp and medusa**; Echinodermata – **water vascular system**.
[3:7:4] - **Agencies of education:** active agencies (two-way interaction) – family, school, community, religious bodies, the State; passive agencies – press, radio, TV, cinema.
[3:7:5] ~~ Sources: Flag Code of India 2002 (mha.gov.in); NCERT Biology Class XI ch.4 (Animal Kingdom); standard B.Ed texts (Gagné, agencies of education)

=== OWN MCQs of 2:3 (70)
[nb4a50bc36d2] sub0
  How does the Indian Constitution describe India?
  *Union of States | Federation of States | Confederation of States | Union of Provinces
  x: India is described as a Union of States with a parliamentary system of government.
[n4f2623651da] sub0
  To which body is the Council of Ministers in India collectively responsible?
  *Lok Sabha | Supreme Court | Rajya Sabha | President alone
  x: The Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha under the parliamentary system.
[nbb0294a9769] sub0
  Why did the framers of the Constitution prefer the parliamentary system?
  India had no experience of representative institutions before 1950 | *it was familiar through the Government of India Acts of 1919 and 1935 | it guarantees a fixed-term executive in every situation | it completely separates the executive from the legislature
  x: The parliamentary system was familiar to India through the Government of India Acts of 1919 and 1935. It was also considered suitable for India's size and diversity and for avoiding executive–legislature conflict.
[n6974eb8880f] sub0
  Unlike the British Parliament, which of the following is true of the Indian Parliament?
  Sovereign in the British sense | *Bound by a written Constitution and judicial review | Headed by a hereditary monarch | Free from the limits of Fundamental Rights
  x: The Indian Parliament is not sovereign in the British sense: it is bound by a written Constitution, Fundamental Rights and judicial review.
[f3e8ba010d058] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT correct about India's parliamentary system?
  Ministers are members of the legislature | *Britain's head of State is elected | India's head of State is elected indirectly | It follows the British model
  x: Britain's head of State is hereditary; it is India's head of State who is elected indirectly.
[n9dea8a330b1] sub0
  India follows which form of democracy?
  Direct democracy | *Representative (indirect) democracy | Direct democracy through referendum and recall | Guided democracy
  x: India follows representative or indirect democracy; referendum, initiative and recall are not part of its national system.
[fadf612ab2c6e] sub0
  Which of the following does India's representative democracy at the national level NOT have?
  Elected representatives | Universal adult franchise | *Referendum, initiative or recall | A parliamentary system
  x: India is a representative (indirect) democracy with no referendum, initiative or recall at the national level.
[gd7c1d8d456a2] sub0
  From which language is the word 'democracy' derived?
  Latin | *Greek | French | Sanskrit
  x: Democracy comes from the Greek words demos (people) and kratos (rule).

  x: India has a parliamentary form of government, not a presidential form. The other pairs correctly describe constitutional features or independent bodies.

  x: New provisions are inserted as lettered Articles such as 21A and 51A, so the original numbering still ends at Article 395.

=== NOTES 2:4 | Preamble – Text, Keywords, 42ND Amendment and Its Status
## SUB 0 | 42nd Amendment, 1976 – Socialist, Secular, Integrity
[4:0:0] # What the 42nd Amendment did to the Preamble
[4:0:1] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [4:0:1.0] Words added | Socialist, Secular, Integrity (three words)
  [4:0:1.1] Description of India | "Sovereign Democratic Republic" → "Sovereign Socialist Secular Democratic Republic"
  [4:0:1.2] Fraternity clause | "unity of the Nation" → "unity and integrity of the Nation"
  [4:0:1.3] Year / context | 1976, during the Emergency (1975-77), Indira Gandhi government
  [4:0:1.4] Based on | Swaran Singh Committee
  [4:0:1.5] Nickname | "Mini Constitution" – it changed the Preamble, the Seventh Schedule and 53 Articles
[4:0:2] - **Only amendment of the Preamble so far:** the **42nd (1976)**; asked about 25 times (APPSC G2 2008, 2011, 2025 Mains; AEE 2008; Endowment 2008; JL 2003; Polytechnic 2019; Degree Lecturers 2020; Constable 2016; SSC, NDA 2018, UPPSC, MPPSC 2023).
[4:0:3] - **⚠ Trap:** not the 1st, 2nd, 17th, 24th, 25th, 38th, 40th, 41st, 43rd, 44th, 45th, 46th, 52nd, 73rd or 86th Amendment ("1976 Second Amendment" is wrong).
[4:0:4] - **'Socialist' and 'Secular' were inserted between 'Sovereign' and 'Democratic'.**
[4:0:5] - **Integrity** was **not** in the Preamble in **1975**; it came in **1976** (SSC 2019; UPPSC 2006). The word **"unity"** was in the **original** text – only "and integrity" was added (a question saying "'Unity of the Nation' was added by the 42nd" means this change).
[4:0:6] - **NOT added by the 42nd:** **Fraternity**, **Sovereign**, Democratic, Republic (all original); **Gram Swarajya**, Dignity of person, Privacy, Liberal are not 42nd words.
[4:0:7] - **Assertion-reason:** "India is a sovereign socialist secular democratic republic" (A) is true and its reason – the 42nd Act **substituted** these words for "Sovereign Democratic Republic" (R) – correctly explains it.
[4:0:8] # Other changes of the 42nd Amendment (for "not associated" items)
[4:0:9] - Added **Fundamental Duties** (Part IVA) and new DPSPs; **curtailed** judicial review in some matters but did **not abolish** it entirely.
[4:0:10] - **Minerva Mills (1980)** struck down its Art. 368 clauses **(4) and (5)** (unlimited amending power, exclusion of judicial review) and the clause giving **DPSPs full priority over all Fundamental Rights** – restoring the balance of Parts III and IV.
[4:0:11] > Status today: in **Dr Balram Singh v. Union of India (25 Nov 2024)** the Supreme Court **upheld 'Socialist' and 'Secular'** and dismissed the challenges to their insertion (as of 2026; the words were again debated politically in Jul 2025).
[4:0:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.11 (42nd Amendment), p.230, p.232; Rocket Polity #14 p.36-37; app notes 2:4 sub 7; sci.gov.in (Balram Singh, 25 Nov 2024)
## SUB 1 | We, the People – Source of Authority, Date and Adoption
[4:1:0] - **Source of the Constitution / of political power:** the **people of India** – the Preamble opens "**We, the People of India**", showing **people's sovereignty**; the Constitution derives its authority from the people (APPSC G1 2002, G2, AEE 2012, CDPO 2013; HWO 2017).
[4:1:1] - **⚠ Trap:** not the Constitution, Parliament, "Parliament and State Legislatures", the President or the Supreme Court.
[4:1:2] - The Preamble **indicates the source** from which the Constitution comes (APPSC G2 2011).
[4:1:3] - **"We, the People of India"** is used in the **Preamble**, not in the FRs, DPSPs or Citizenship part.
[4:1:4] - **Enacting clause:** "We, the people of India … **in our Constituent Assembly** … do hereby **adopt, enact and give to ourselves** this Constitution" – the Constitution is declared adopted and enacted by the **people of India**, acting through the **Constituent Assembly** (when only the CA is offered, it is the answer for "adopted by").
[4:1:5] # The date
[4:1:6] - **Date in the Preamble:** **26 November 1949** = **adoption** of the Constitution (drafting completed; signed by the President of the CA) – not 26 January 1950 (commencement), 24 January 1950 or 13 December 1946.
[4:1:7] - **Commencement:** **26 January 1950** (some provisions – citizenship, elections, provisional Parliament – took effect on 26 Nov 1949).
[4:1:8] - **Constitution Day:** **26 November**, observed **since 2015** (Constitution Day 2019 = 26 Nov) (APPSC G2 Mains 2017; JL 2020).
[4:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.136-137, p.229-230; Rocket Polity #14 p.36-37; app notes 2:4 sub 0-1
## SUB 2 | Is the Preamble Part of the Constitution? – Cases and Legal Status
[4:2:0] TABLE head: Case | Year | Held on the Preamble
  [4:2:0.0] Berubari Union (President's reference) | 1960 | Not a part of the Constitution (the first such ruling); a "key to the minds of the makers"; aid to interpretation where an Article is ambiguous
  [4:2:0.1] Golaknath | 1967 | About amending Fundamental Rights; not the Preamble case (distractor)
  [4:2:0.2] Kesavananda Bharati | 1973 | Is a part of the Constitution (overruled Berubari); amendable under Art. 368 but the basic structure cannot be destroyed; 13 judges
  [4:2:0.3] S.R. Bommai | 1994 | Relied on the Preamble – secularism a basic feature; Preamble treated as integral part
  [4:2:0.4] LIC of India | 1995 | Reaffirmed the Preamble is an integral part
  [4:2:0.5] Minerva Mills | 1980 | Struck down 42nd Amendment Art. 368 clauses – not a Preamble-status case
[4:2:1] - **"Preamble is a part" =** **Kesavananda Bharati** (APPSC TWO 2012, G2 2008, ZSWO 2025); with no Kesavananda option, **Bommai** (UPPSC 2012); A.K. Gopalan, Maneka Gandhi, Mohini Jain, Sajjan Singh are distractors.
[4:2:2] - **"Not a part" =** **Berubari, 1960** (not 1968; Kesavananda was 1973, not 1970).
[4:2:3] # Legal nature
[4:2:4] - **Non-justiciable** – **not enforceable** in a court of law (UPPSC 2019).
[4:2:5] - **Neither a source of power** to the legislature **nor a prohibition** on its powers; confers no power and creates no right by itself.
[4:2:6] - **Part of the Constitution but has no legal effect independently of other parts** (UPSC 2020).
[4:2:7] - **Cannot override** the specific provisions of the Constitution; where a provision is clear, the Preamble yields.
[4:2:8] - **Aid to interpretation:** may be invoked to determine the **ambit of Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles** (APPSC G1).
[4:2:9] - **Amendable** under Art. 368 (done once, 1976) – so "it cannot be amended" is false; "not a part of the Constitution" is false.
[4:2:10] - **Enacted after** the rest of the Constitution, so that it conformed to the text – "enacted before the entire Constitution" is false (APPSC G1 2023 → I and III incorrect).
[4:2:11] - Reflects the **philosophy, ideals and objectives** of the Constitution – the **mind of the makers** (UPSC 2017) and the **objective of the Constitution** (UPPSC 2013) are reflected in the Preamble, not in FRs, DPSP or FDs.
[4:2:12] > Statement sets: "Objectives Resolution became the Preamble ✓; non-justiciable ✓; can't be amended ✗; can't override specific provisions ✓" → **I, II and IV** (UPPSC 2009, Panchayat Secretary). "Not a part ✗; amended only under Art. 368 ✓; three words added in 1976 ✓" → **B and C**.
[4:2:13] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.159 (cases), p.229 (status); Rocket Polity #14 p.37; app notes 2:4 sub 4-5
[4:2:14] - **S.R. Bommai (1994):** a State government acting against **secularism** can be dismissed under Art. 356.
[4:2:15] - **Waman Rao (1981):** basic-structure review applies to amendments made **after 24 April 1973** (the Kesavananda judgment date).
[4:2:16] - **Secularism** was part of the basic structure **even before** the word 'Secular' was inserted in 1976 (Kesavananda 1973; Bommai 1994).
## SUB 3 | Preamble Text – Order of Words, Original 1949 Text, Words Not in It
[4:3:0] # The text (as amended in 1976)
[4:3:1] - **WE, THE PEOPLE OF INDIA**, having solemnly resolved to constitute India into a **SOVEREIGN SOCIALIST SECULAR DEMOCRATIC REPUBLIC** and to secure to all its citizens: **JUSTICE**, social, economic and political; **LIBERTY** of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship; **EQUALITY** of status and of opportunity; and to promote among them all **FRATERNITY** assuring the dignity of the individual and the unity and integrity of the Nation; IN OUR CONSTITUENT ASSEMBLY this twenty-sixth day of November, 1949, do HEREBY ADOPT, ENACT AND GIVE TO OURSELVES THIS CONSTITUTION.
[4:3:2] - **Order:** **Sovereign → Socialist → Secular → Democratic → Republic** (code C, A, D, B for Socialist A, Democratic B, Sovereign C, Secular D); objectives in order **Justice → Liberty → Equality → Fraternity**.
[4:3:3] - **India is declared secular by the Preamble** (Fundamental Rights, DPSP and 9th Schedule do not "describe" India as secular).
[4:3:4] - **Equality of** → **status** (and of opportunity); not "of nationality", "religion" or "community".
[4:3:5] # Original 1949 text vs now
[4:3:6] TABLE head: Item | 26 Nov 1949 / 26 Jan 1950 | Now (after 1976)
  [4:3:6.0] Description of India | "Sovereign Democratic Republic" | "Sovereign Socialist Secular Democratic Republic"
  [4:3:6.1] Fraternity clause | "unity of the Nation" | "unity and integrity of the Nation"
  [4:3:6.2] Words absent in 1949 | Socialist, Secular, Integrity | added
[4:3:7] - **Exact status on 26 January 1950:** a **Sovereign Democratic Republic** (UPSC 2021).
[4:3:8] - **Not in the 1949 Preamble:** Socialism / Socialist, Secular, Integrity; "Reliable" and "Non-religious" were never in it; **Republic** was in the original (UPPSC 2009; Uttarakhand 2024 → 2, 3, 4).
[4:3:9] # Words NOT in the Preamble at all
[4:3:10] - **Federal / federation** (APPSC Deputy Surveyors 2012; UPPSC 2021) – "Quasi-federal" is not in it either.
[4:3:11] - **Freedom** (it uses **Liberty**), **Right** (as a word), **Public Welfare** / **Welfare State**, **Adult franchise**, **Fundamental Duties**, "**People's**".
[4:3:12] - **Religious Justice**, **Natural** or **Cultural** justice (justice is only **social, economic, political**).
[4:3:13] - **Political Liberty** and **Economic Liberty** as categories; liberty "of action" (liberty is only of **thought, expression, belief, faith, worship**) (UPSC 2017; APPSC G2).
[4:3:14] - Words that ARE in it: Fraternity, Equality, Justice, **Unity**, Integrity, Thought, Opportunity, **Dignity**, Republic, Sovereign.
[4:3:15] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.229-230 (text, 42nd words); Rocket Polity #14 p.36-37; app notes 2:4 sub 1
## SUB 4 | Keywords and Objectives – Meaning
[4:4:0] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [4:4:0.0] Sovereign | Supreme authority; free internally and externally; not subject to any external authority
  [4:4:0.1] Socialist | Democratic socialism – a mixed economy (public and private sectors); a blend of Marxism and Gandhism, leaning towards Gandhian socialism
  [4:4:0.2] Secular | Equal respect for all religions; no State religion; before 1976 the word appeared only in Art. 25(2)(a); the term is not defined in any Article
  [4:4:0.3] Democratic | Government by the people; representative parliamentary democracy; covers political, social and economic democracy; universal adult franchise and rule of law are its marks
  [4:4:0.4] Republic | Elected head of State (the President, indirectly elected for 5 years); no hereditary element; public offices open to all
[4:4:1] - **Republic ≠ PM:** India is a republic because its **head of State (President) is elected** – not because ministers are responsible to Parliament or hold office at the President's pleasure.
[4:4:2] - **Philosophy of the Constitution:** welfare state, socialist state, political equality – but **not a Communist State** (UPPSC 2015).
[4:4:3] - India is a **democratic polity, a sovereign State, a democratic society and a welfare state** – all four (UPPSC 2012); the **welfare-state** idea is found in the **Preamble and the DPSP** (UPPSC 1997).
[4:4:4] - **Not valid (APPSC G1 2024):** "'democratic' is a mix of British Fabian Socialism and American Populism"; "Republic = elected Prime Minister"; "Liberty is neither absolute nor qualified"; "Fraternity originally denoted only dignity of the individual" → **none valid**.
[4:4:5] # Objectives
[4:4:6] TABLE head: Objective | Of / kinds
  [4:4:6.0] Justice | Social, economic and political – three kinds
  [4:4:6.1] Liberty | Thought, expression, belief, faith and worship – five
  [4:4:6.2] Equality | Status and opportunity – civic, political, economic dimensions
  [4:4:6.3] Fraternity | Sense of brotherhood; assures dignity of the individual and unity and integrity of the Nation; promoted by single citizenship
[4:4:7] - **Goals secured to all:** justice (social, economic, political), liberty of thought and expression, equality of opportunity and **dignity of the individual** – **all** of them (UPPSC 1998).
[4:4:8] - **Liberty** = **absence of restraints plus opportunity to develop one's personality**; UPSC 2019 "most appropriate definition" = **opportunity to develop oneself fully** (positive liberty); liberty is not licence to do whatever one likes.
[4:4:9] - **Law and liberty (UPSC 2018):** "**If there are no laws, there is no liberty**" – law is the condition of liberty.
[4:4:10] - **Political theory links:** popular/political **sovereignty** – **Rousseau** (general will); **distributive justice** – **Aristotle**.
[4:4:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.166 (secular state), p.230-232 (keywords, justice, liberty, equality, fraternity); Rocket Polity #14 p.36; app notes 2:4 sub 2-3; UPSC CSE 2018, 2019 official keys
[4:4:12] - **Kinds of justice:** economic factors are treated as a source of justice (**economic justice**); **social justice** = equal treatment of all, **equality before law**, no discrimination by caste, creed or sex; **political justice** = **political equality** (equal political rights and voice) – not inequality.
## SUB 5 | Making and Sources of the Preamble's Ideals
[4:5:0] - **Basis:** the **Objectives Resolution** of **Jawaharlal Nehru** – moved **13 December 1946**, adopted **22 January 1947**; its modified version became the Preamble (Nehru "proposed the Preamble"; not Ambedkar, B.N. Rau or Gandhi) (APPSC ASWO; Uttarakhand 2006; APPSC JA).
[4:5:1] - The Constitution of India was **not** the first to begin with a Preamble (the **US** Constitution did); the Preamble has been amended **once**, not twice.
[4:5:2] TABLE head: Element | Source
  [4:5:2.0] Idea of a Preamble; "We, the people" | USA
  [4:5:2.1] Language of the Preamble | Australia
  [4:5:2.2] Liberty, Equality, Fraternity (the "trinity"); Republic | French Revolution (1789) / French Constitution
  [4:5:2.3] Justice – social, economic and political | Russian Revolution (1917), USSR
  [4:5:2.4] Fraternity (possible) | Art. 1 of the UDHR (1948)
[4:5:3] - **⚠ Trap:** Liberty-equality-fraternity is **not** from the Russian, American or Glorious Revolution or the UN Charter; justice is **not** from the French Revolution.
[4:5:4] - **Third reading** of the Constitution: **17-26 November 1949**; Preamble enacted last.
[4:5:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.140, p.152 (Q.14), p.155 (sources table), p.229, p.231; Rocket Polity #14 p.36; app notes 2:4 sub 0
## SUB 6 | Descriptions of the Preamble – Who Said What
[4:6:0] TABLE head: Description | Person
  [4:6:0.0] "Identity card of the Constitution" | N.A. Palkhivala
  [4:6:0.1] "Political horoscope" / "horoscope of our sovereign democratic republic" | K.M. Munshi
  [4:6:0.2] "Key-note" to the Constitution | Ernest Barker
  [4:6:0.3] "Key to the minds of the makers" | Supreme Court, Berubari (1960)
  [4:6:0.4] "Soul" of the Constitution | Thakurdas Bhargava (the Preamble is called the soul of the Constitution)
  [4:6:0.5] "Heart and soul" | Ambedkar – Article 32, not the Preamble
[4:6:1] - **"Key of the Constitution"** – the standard attributions are **Ernest Barker** ("key-note") and the **Supreme Court** (Berubari, Sajjan Singh: "key to open the mind of the makers"); no reliable source gives it to Gandhi, Motilal Nehru, Ambedkar or Indira Gandhi.
[4:6:2] - **Critic of inserting 'Secular' (1976):** constitutional scholar **D.D. Basu** held the insertion unnecessary and productive of vagueness – "productive of more mischief than benefit" (owner to confirm against Basu's *Introduction to the Constitution of India*).
[4:6:3] ~~ Sources: Rocket Polity #14 p.37; PMF Polity Part 1 p.42 (Palkhivala); app notes 2:4 sub 8; blog.lukmaanias.com (Preamble, 2025: Basu on 'secular')

[nindian-polity-14-11] sub4
  What does secularism mean in the Indian context?
  Hostility to all religions | A State religion with tolerance of others | Rejection of all religious practices | *Equal respect for all religions
  x: Secularism in India means equal respect for all religions.
[ff8a1c1859319] sub4
  What does the term 'Sovereign' in the Preamble mean for India?
  *Free internally and externally | Ruled by an elected head | Committed to no State religion | A mixed economy
  x: Sovereign means supreme authority, internally and externally free; the others describe Republic, Secular and Socialist.
[f275c3c46c370] sub4
  Towards which form of socialism does the 'Socialist' character of the Indian State lean?
  Marxian socialism | Fabian socialism | *Gandhian socialism | State communism
  x: Indian socialism is democratic socialism, a blend of Marxism and Gandhism leaning towards Gandhian socialism.
[n70192500fde] sub4
  Equality of what does the Preamble assure?
  *Status and opportunity | Rights and opportunity | Income and status | Status and representation
  x: The Preamble assures equality of status and opportunity, along with liberty of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship.
[n0275f2b6c15] sub4
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the objectives in the Preamble?
  Economic justice means removing economic discrimination and glaring inequality. | *Liberty means the complete absence of every restraint on individual activity. | Political justice means equal political rights and access to office. | Equality has civic, political and economic dimensions.
  x: Liberty means absence of restraints plus opportunities to develop one's personality, not freedom from every restraint.
[nindian-polity-14-10] sub4
  Which of the following pairs from the Preamble is correctly matched?
  *Justice – Social, economic and political | Liberty – Of status and of opportunity | Equality – Of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship | Fraternity – Of status and of opportunity
  x: Justice is social, economic and political; liberty is of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship; equality is of status and opportunity.
[f05ea0d31cc4c] sub4
  How many kinds of liberty does the Preamble name?
  Three | Four | *Five | Six
  x: Liberty of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship – five kinds.
[f0fd29158c3fa] sub4
  Which of the following promotes Fraternity in the Indian context?
  *Single citizenship | Dual citizenship | Separate State constitutions | Bicameral legislatures
  x: Fraternity, a sense of brotherhood, is promoted by single citizenship.
[f8493227ac35e] sub4
  What does Liberty mean in the Preamble?
  Freedom from every restraint | *Absence of restraints plus opportunities to develop personality | Freedom of action alone | Economic liberty
  x: Liberty means absence of restraints on individual activity plus opportunities to develop personality, not freedom from every restraint.
[ga163302a933c] sub4
  Which of the following are the three dimensions of equality in the Preamble's scheme?
  *Civic, political and economic | Social, religious and political | Status, wealth and opportunity | Legal, moral and cultural
  x: Equality in the Preamble has three dimensions: civic, political and economic.
[nf039025cfa8] sub5
  Of which document is the Preamble of the Constitution a modified version?
  *Objectives Resolution | Cabinet Mission Plan | Government of India Act, 1935 | Indian Independence Act, 1947
  x: The Preamble is a modified version of the Objectives Resolution moved by Jawaharlal Nehru and adopted by the Constituent Assembly.
[nindian-polity-14-1] sub5
  On which date was the Objectives Resolution, on which the Preamble was based, adopted?
  *22 January 1947 | 26 November 1949 | 26 January 1947 | 13 December 1946
  x: Nehru moved the Objectives Resolution on 13 December 1946 and it was adopted on 22 January 1947; 26 November 1949 is the date of adoption of the Constitution.
[n4bd5a3d9e25] sub5
  From the Constitution of which country was the idea of having a Preamble taken?
  Australia | *USA | Russia | France
  x: The idea of a Preamble and 'We, the people' come from the US Constitution; its language is credited to Australia.
[f00c031e3c77b] sub5
  Who moved the Objectives Resolution, the basis of the Preamble?
  B.R. Ambedkar | *Jawaharlal Nehru | Rajendra Prasad | K.M. Munshi
  x: Jawaharlal Nehru moved the Objectives Resolution on 13 December 1946; its modified version became the Preamble.
[f68b402818ffb] sub5
  On which date was the Objectives Resolution moved in the Constituent Assembly?
  9 December 1946 | *13 December 1946 | 22 January 1947 | 26 November 1949
  x: It was moved on 13 December 1946 and adopted unanimously on 22 January 1947.
[f2de6f2e7b536] sub5
  From which of the following is the ideal of Justice – social, economic and political – in the Preamble drawn?
  French Revolution | American Revolution | *Russian Revolution | UDHR
  x: Justice (social, economic, political) comes from the Russian Revolution (1917); Liberty, Equality, Fraternity from the French Revolution (1789).
[f1af3c90df753] sub5
  The language of the Preamble is credited to the Constitution of which country?
  USA | *Australia | Canada | Ireland
  x: The language of the Preamble is credited to Australia; the idea of a Preamble comes from the USA.
[g6e965ec5046e] sub5
  From which source were the ideals of Liberty, Equality and Fraternity in the Preamble taken?
  Russian Revolution | American Constitution | *French Revolution | Irish Constitution
  x: Liberty, Equality and Fraternity come from the French Revolution of 1789.

  x: The idea of a Preamble is from the USA, its language from Australia, social-economic-political justice from the Russian Revolution and Liberty-Equality-Fraternity from the French Revolution.

  x: Thakurdas Bhargava called the Preamble the soul of the Constitution; Ambedkar's 'heart and soul' was Article 32.

=== NOTES 2:5 | Parts, Schedules and Articles of the Constitution
## SUB 0 | Eighth Schedule – Languages
[5:0:0] # The 22 scheduled languages
[5:0:1] - **Eighth Schedule = languages recognised by the Constitution** (Arts. **344(1) and 351**) – the "regional languages" / "official languages" schedule; **not** the Fourth, Sixth, Seventh or Ninth.
[5:0:2] - **Number:** originally **14**, now **22** (not 18, 21 or 24).
[5:0:3] - **The 22:** Assamese, Bengali, **Bodo**, **Dogri**, Gujarati, Hindi, Kannada, **Kashmiri**, **Konkani**, **Maithili**, Malayalam, **Manipuri**, Marathi, **Nepali**, Odia, Punjabi, Sanskrit, **Santhali**, **Sindhi**, Tamil, Telugu, **Urdu**.
[5:0:4] - **NOT in the Eighth Schedule:** **English**, **Rajasthani**, **Bhojpuri**, **Gondi**, Bhutia (Bhoti), Tulu, Chhattisgarhi, Gor Boli (Lambadi) – all asked as the odd one out.
[5:0:5] - Hindi, Sanskrit, Urdu, Punjabi, Gujarati, Kashmiri were among the **original 14**.
[5:0:6] # Languages added by amendment
[5:0:7] TABLE head: Amendment | Year | Languages added
  [5:0:7.0] 21st | 1967 | Sindhi
  [5:0:7.1] 71st | 1992 | Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali
  [5:0:7.2] 92nd | 2003 | Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, Santhali
[5:0:8] - **71st ≠ Maithili:** Maithili came with the **92nd** (UPSC 2024: 71st = Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali only).
[5:0:9] - **Konkani** entered in **1992** (not 2003). 96th Amendment (2011) renamed Oriya as **Odia** (no new language).
[5:0:10] - In **1990** Sindhi was the only language added by amendment; since 1992 Konkani, Manipuri and Nepali also are.
[5:0:11] # Official Language Commission (Art. 344)
[5:0:12] - First **Official Language Commission** set up **7 June 1955** under Art. 344 – chairman **B.G. Kher**; report 1956, reviewed by a parliamentary committee under **G.B. Pant**; **Official Languages Act 1963**.
[5:0:13] - Official language provisions = **Part XVII, Arts. 343–351**.
[5:0:14] # Demands for inclusion (current linkage)
[5:0:15] - No fixed criteria for inclusion (**Pahwa Committee 1996**, **Sitakant Mohapatra Committee 2003**); demands are raised in Parliament – e.g. **Chhattisgarhi** in the Rajya Sabha (CGPSC 2019, 2020) → the **8th Schedule**.
[5:0:16] - **Jun 2026**: fresh demands to include **Bhojpuri** (Konkani, Maithili, Santali are already in).
[5:0:17] - **Jul 2026**: Ministry of Tribal Affairs told Parliament of continuing demands to include **Gor Boli (Lambadi)**, the **Banjara** language of AP and Telangana (PIB).
[5:0:18] - **Sep 2026**: Karnataka made **Tulu** an additional official language in Dakshina Kannada and Udupi – Tulu is **not** in the Eighth Schedule.
[5:0:19] > Odd-one-out pattern: English (3 times), Rajasthani, Gondi, Bhojpuri, Bhutani – none is scheduled; Urdu, Sindhi, Nepali, Dogri, Kashmiri, Sanskrit are.
[5:0:20] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.157; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.2 (8th Schedule list); PMF Lens Jul 2026 1.23; Praveen CA360 Sep 2026 (Tulu); Official Languages Commission – Lok Sabha debates 5 May 1955 and Report 1956
[5:0:21] - **English as a State official language:** **Nagaland** (also Meghalaya and Arunachal Pradesh) – though English is not in the Eighth Schedule.
[5:0:22] - **Official-language Articles:** **Art. 343** – **Hindi in Devanagari** = official language of the **Union** (from **26 Jan 1950**; adopted 14 Sep 1949), English for 15 years; **Art. 345** – a **State legislature** may adopt Hindi or any language of the State; **Art. 346** – Centre–State / inter-State communication; **Art. 348** – language of the SC, HCs and laws.
[5:0:23] - **Official Languages Act, 1963 (amended 1967):** English may continue alongside Hindi for official Union purposes **indefinitely** (beyond the 15 years of Art. 343(2)).
## SUB 1 | The Twelve Schedules – Contents
[5:1:0] # Schedule → contents → Articles
[5:1:1] TABLE head: Schedule | Contents | Articles
  [5:1:1.0] First | Names of the States and Union Territories and their territories | 1 and 4
  [5:1:1.1] Second | Emoluments, allowances, privileges – President, Governors, Speakers/Chairmen, Judges, CAG | 59, 65, 75, 97, 125, 148, 158, 164, 186, 221
  [5:1:1.2] Third | Forms of oaths and affirmations | 75, 84, 99, 124, 148, 164, 173, 188, 219
  [5:1:1.3] Fourth | Allocation of seats in the Rajya Sabha (Council of States) to States and UTs | 4 and 80
  [5:1:1.4] Fifth | Administration and control of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes | 244
  [5:1:1.5] Sixth | Tribal areas of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram | 244 and 275
  [5:1:1.6] Seventh | Division of powers – Union, State and Concurrent Lists | 246
  [5:1:1.7] Eighth | Languages – 22 | 344 and 351
  [5:1:1.8] Ninth | Acts protected from judicial review (land reforms etc.) | 31B
  [5:1:1.9] Tenth | Anti-defection – disqualification on the ground of defection | 102 and 191
  [5:1:1.10] Eleventh | Powers of Panchayats – 29 matters | 243G
  [5:1:1.11] Twelfth | Powers of Municipalities – 18 matters | 243W
[5:1:2] - **Match (APSLPRB SI 2017):** 7th = distribution of powers, 10th = anti-defection, 11th = Panchayats, 12th = Municipalities.
[5:1:3] - **Wrong pairs asked:** "Ninth – allocation of seats in the Council of States" (that is the **Fourth**); "Eleventh – Municipalities" (that is the **Twelfth**; the Eleventh = Panchayats).
[5:1:4] - **New State created → First Schedule changes** (names and territories; the Fourth changes too when Rajya Sabha seats are re-allotted).
[5:1:5] - **PESA districts / Scheduled Areas** are identified under the **Fifth Schedule** (PESA 1996 extends Part IX to Fifth Schedule areas); the Sixth covers the four north-eastern States.
[5:1:6] # Schedules added by amendment
[5:1:7] TABLE head: Schedule | Added by
  [5:1:7.0] 9th | 1st Amendment, 1951 – first added
  [5:1:7.1] 10th | 52nd Amendment, 1985
  [5:1:7.2] 11th | 73rd Amendment, 1992 (with Part IX)
  [5:1:7.3] 12th | 74th Amendment, 1992 (with Part IXA) – last added
[5:1:8] # Third Schedule – oaths
[5:1:9] - Covers: Union and State **ministers**; candidates for and members of **Parliament** and **State legislatures**; Judges of the **Supreme Court** and **High Courts**; the **CAG**.
[5:1:10] - "Bear true faith and allegiance … uphold the sovereignty and integrity of India … **perform the duties of my office without fear or favour** … **uphold the Constitution and the laws**" = Form IV – **Judges of the Supreme Court (incl. the Chief Justice of India) and the CAG**. The President's oath says "preserve, protect and defend"; an MP's "faithfully discharge the duty".
[5:1:11] - **Outside the Third Schedule:** President – **Art. 60**; Vice-President – **Art. 69**; Governor – **Art. 159**.
[5:1:12] # Seventh Schedule – three Lists
[5:1:13] TABLE head: List | Originally | Now
  [5:1:13.0] Union | 97 | 100
  [5:1:13.1] State | 66 | 61
  [5:1:13.2] Concurrent | 47 | 52
[5:1:14] - Changes came mainly through the **42nd Amendment (1976)** (items moved from the State to the Concurrent List). Strictly counted after the **101st Amendment (GST)** the Union List has 98 and the State List 59 entries; the standard exam figures are 100 and 61.
[5:1:15] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.156-157, p.266-267; Rocket Polity Part 1 p.16-17, p.30 (Fifth Schedule); Constitution of India, Third Schedule Form IV (legislative.gov.in)
## SUB 2 | Ninth Schedule – Land Reforms and Judicial Review
[5:2:0] # Basics
[5:2:1] - Added by the **First Amendment Act, 1951** – the **first Schedule added** – during the prime ministership of **Jawaharlal Nehru** (not the 8th, 9th or 42nd Amendment).
[5:2:2] - Linked with **Article 31B**: Acts placed in it cannot be challenged for violating Fundamental Rights.
[5:2:3] - Purpose: to protect **state land-reform and zamindari-abolition laws** from judicial scrutiny – so **land reform laws are in the 9th Schedule** (not the 7th, 8th or 10th).
[5:2:4] - Acts: **13** originally → about **284** now (NextGen counts 282).
[5:2:5] - **I.R. Coelho (2007):** laws added **after 24 April 1973** (the Kesavananda date) are **open to judicial review** on the basic-structure test.
[5:2:6] # Tamil Nadu 69% reservation
[5:2:7] - The **Tamil Nadu Reservation Act, 1993/94** (69% reservation for SCs, STs and OBCs – above the 50% Indra Sawhney limit) was placed in the **IX Schedule** by the **76th Amendment Act, 1994** to shield it from judicial review.
[5:2:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.157, p.217, p.261; Rocket Polity Part 1 p.17; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.5 p.90 (76th Amendment)
## SUB 3 | Parts of the Constitution
[5:3:0] # The 25 Parts
[5:3:1] TABLE head: Part | Subject | Articles
  [5:3:1.0] I | Union and its Territory | 1–4
  [5:3:1.1] II | Citizenship | 5–11
  [5:3:1.2] III | Fundamental Rights | 12–35
  [5:3:1.3] IV | Directive Principles | 36–51
  [5:3:1.4] IV-A | Fundamental Duties (42nd, 1976) | 51-A
  [5:3:1.5] V | The Union – Executive, Parliament, Judiciary, CAG | 52–151
  [5:3:1.6] VI | The States | 152–237
  [5:3:1.7] VII | States in Part B of the First Schedule – repealed (7th Amendment, 1956) | —
  [5:3:1.8] VIII | Union Territories | 239–242
  [5:3:1.9] IX | Panchayats (73rd, 1992) | 243–243-O
  [5:3:1.10] IX-A | Municipalities (74th, 1992) | 243-P–243-ZG
  [5:3:1.11] IX-B | Co-operative Societies (97th, 2011) | 243-ZH–243-ZT
  [5:3:1.12] X | Scheduled and Tribal Areas | 244–244-A
  [5:3:1.13] XI | Relations between the Union and the States | 245–263
  [5:3:1.14] XII | Finance, Property, Contracts and Suits | 264–300-A
  [5:3:1.15] XIII | Trade, Commerce and Intercourse | 301–307
  [5:3:1.16] XIV | Services under the Union and the States (incl. Public Service Commissions, Arts. 315–323) | 308–323
  [5:3:1.17] XIV-A | Tribunals (42nd, 1976) | 323-A–323-B
  [5:3:1.18] XV | Elections | 324–329-A
  [5:3:1.19] XVI | Special provisions relating to certain classes (SC/ST/Anglo-Indian seats, commissions) | 330–342
  [5:3:1.20] XVII | Official Language | 343–351
  [5:3:1.21] XVIII | Emergency | 352–360
  [5:3:1.22] XIX | Miscellaneous | 361–367
  [5:3:1.23] XX | Amendment | 368 only
  [5:3:1.24] XXI | Temporary, Transitional and Special Provisions (Art. 370, 371–371J) | 369–392
  [5:3:1.25] XXII | Short title, commencement, Hindi text, repeals | 393–395
[5:3:2] - **Wrong pair asked:** "Part XX – Municipalities" (Part XX = Amendment; Municipalities = **Part IX-A**).
[5:3:3] - **73rd Amendment, 1992 → new Part IX** (Panchayats) + Eleventh Schedule; **74th → Part IX-A (Municipalities)** + Twelfth Schedule – not district boards or townships. Parts X and XI were in the original Constitution.
[5:3:4] - **Part IX-B:** in 2021 (*Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah*) the SC struck it down **for State co-operative societies**; it survives for **multi-State co-operatives** – the Part is still counted.
[5:3:5] - Part XIV-A came by the **42nd Amendment (1976)** – which also added Part IV-A – not the 44th.
[5:3:6] # Deleted Parts and Articles
[5:3:7] TABLE head: Provision | What happened
  [5:3:7.0] Part VII (Part B States) | Repealed by the 7th Amendment, 1956 (not the 44th) – now the only vacant Part number (the old Part IX was reused in 1992)
  [5:3:7.1] Art. 2A (Sikkim as associate State) | Inserted by the 35th, omitted by the 36th Amendment
  [5:3:7.2] Art. 329A | Omitted by the 44th Amendment
  [5:3:7.3] Art. 242 (Part VIII) | Repealed
  [5:3:7.4] Right to Property (Arts. 31, 19(1)(f)) | Deleted by the 44th; now Art. 300A – a legal right
[5:3:8] ~~ Sources: Rocket Polity Part 1 p.18-19 (Rocket Sheet #6); NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.158; Rocket Polity Part 1 p.57-58 (Part IX-B); SC, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah (20 Jul 2021)
## SUB 4 | Tenth Schedule – Anti-defection
[5:4:0] - **Anti-defection law = Tenth Schedule** (Arts. **102(2) and 191(2)**) – not the 2nd, 5th, 6th, 7th, 8th or 9th.
[5:4:1] - Added by the **52nd Amendment Act, 1985** (Rajiv Gandhi government) – not the 42nd, 50th, 51st, 53rd, 85th or 95th.
[5:4:2] - **91st Amendment (2003)** removed the **'split'** (one-third) exception; **merger** (two-thirds) still protected; the presiding officer decides disqualification (subject to judicial review – *Kihoto Hollohan*, 1992).
[5:4:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.59, p.157; Rocket Polity Part 1 p.17
## SUB 5 | Special Provisions – Articles 371 to 371J
[5:5:0] TABLE head: Article | State | Special provision
  [5:5:0.0] 371 | Maharashtra and Gujarat | Development boards – Vidarbha, Marathwada, Saurashtra, Kutch
  [5:5:0.1] 371A | Nagaland | Naga customs, customary law, land – Parliament's laws apply only if the Assembly agrees
  [5:5:0.2] 371B | Assam | Assembly committee of members from tribal areas
  [5:5:0.3] 371C | Manipur | Assembly committee of members from the Hill areas
  [5:5:0.4] 371D | Andhra Pradesh (and Telangana) | Equitable opportunities in public employment and education for regions
  [5:5:0.5] 371E | Andhra Pradesh | Central University by parliamentary law
  [5:5:0.6] 371F | Sikkim | Incorporation, assembly size, governance
  [5:5:0.7] 371G | Mizoram | Mizo practices, customary law, land
  [5:5:0.8] 371H | Arunachal Pradesh | Governor's special responsibility for law and order
  [5:5:0.9] 371I | Goa | Assembly at least 30 members (usual minimum 60)
  [5:5:0.10] 371J | Karnataka | Hyderabad–Karnataka region (six backward districts) – development board, reservation; 98th Amendment, 2012
[5:5:1] - All are in **Part XXI**; **Art. 371** is "the" special-provisions Article (not 369, 370 or 372). Art. 370 (J&K) is not in the 371 series.
[5:5:2] - **Arts. 371D and 371E** came by the **32nd Amendment, 1973** (Six-Point Formula after the Jai Andhra and Telangana agitations); the 32nd also dropped the old AP clause from Art. 371.
[5:5:3] - **North-East trap:** 371A Nagaland, **371B Assam**, 371C Manipur.
[5:5:4] ~~ Sources: Rocket Polity Part 2 p.8-9 (Rocket Sheet #48); PMF Polity Part 3 (Arts. 371–371J)
## SUB 6 | Counts – Original vs Present (as of 2026)
[5:6:0] TABLE head: Item | Original (26 Nov 1949) | Now (2026)
  [5:6:0.0] Articles | 395 (with the Preamble) | about 470 – numbering still ends at 395; new ones lettered (243ZH–243ZT, 330A, 334A …)
  [5:6:0.1] Parts | 22 | 25
  [5:6:0.2] Schedules | 8 | 12
[5:6:1] - **Parts:** 22 + 4 added (**IV-A, IX-A, IX-B, XIV-A**) − Part VII repealed = **25** (Part IX of 1950 was repealed in 1956 and a new Part IX inserted in 1992). Some books still print 24 Parts or "447 Articles" – outdated.
[5:6:2] - **Schedules:** 8 + 4 added (**9th–12th**) = **12** (not 9, 10, 11 or 14).
[5:6:3] - Latest enacted amendment as of 2026: **106th (2023)** – women's reservation (Arts. 239AA, 330A, 332A, 334A); no amendment has added a Part or Schedule since 2011.
[5:6:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.118, p.142, p.160; Rocket Polity Part 1 p.18; Indian_Polity Part 1 ch.2 (470 Articles, 25 Parts); PIB via SuperKalam 26 Jul 2026 (amendments to the 106th)
## SUB 7 | Key Articles – Number Game
[5:7:0] TABLE head: Article | Subject
  [5:7:0.0] 1 | "India, that is Bharat, shall be a Union of States"
  [5:7:0.1] 32 | Right to constitutional remedies
  [5:7:0.2] 110 | Definition of a Money Bill
  [5:7:0.3] 123 | Ordinance power of the President
  [5:7:0.4] 143 | Advisory jurisdiction of the SC
  [5:7:0.5] 280 | Finance Commission
  [5:7:0.6] 300A | Right to property – legal right
  [5:7:0.7] 312 | All-India Services
  [5:7:0.8] 315–323 | Public Service Commissions (320 = functions)
  [5:7:0.9] 324 | Election Commission
  [5:7:0.10] 326 | Adult suffrage (elections on the basis of adult suffrage)
  [5:7:0.11] 330–334 | Reservation of seats – SCs, STs (and the Anglo-Indian nomination of Arts. 331/333, lapsed in Jan 2020 – 104th Amendment)
  [5:7:0.12] 338 / 338A / 338B | NCSC / NCST / NCBC
  [5:7:0.13] 340 | Commission for Backward Classes
  [5:7:0.14] 343–351 | Official Language
  [5:7:0.15] 356 | President's Rule
  [5:7:0.16] 368 | Amendment of the Constitution
  [5:7:0.17] 370 | Temporary provisions for J&K – made inoperative in 2019
[5:7:1] - **Match (UPPSC 2023):** 324 – Election Commission, 315 – PSC, 280 – Finance Commission, 338 – NCSC.
[5:7:2] - **Reorganisation of States, 1956 = 7th Amendment Act** (abolished Part A/B/C/D States, created States and UTs; repealed Part VII).
[5:7:3] - **Right to Property** omitted as a Fundamental Right by the **44th Amendment (1978)** – not the 40th, 42nd or 46th.
[5:7:4] # Article 1 – Union of States
[5:7:5] - India is a **"Union"** – not the result of an agreement among States; **States cannot secede**.
[5:7:6] - "**An indestructible Union of destructible States**".
[5:7:7] ~~ Sources: Rocket Polity Part 1 p.10-11 (article list), p.38-40 (44th); NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams p.158

2. No State has the right to secede from it.
