  x: Termination is automatic on voluntarily acquiring foreign citizenship; renunciation is Section 8 and fraud leads to deprivation.
[fdf20e0a2b311] sub6
  What does Section 7A of the Citizenship Act, 1955 deal with?
  *Registration of OCI cardholders | National Register of Indian Citizens | Power to make Rules | Citizenship by naturalisation
  x: Section 7A covers registration of OCI cardholders; Section 14A is the NRIC and Section 18 the rule-making power.
[gba60db3211ad] sub6
  How many modes of losing Indian citizenship are laid down in the Citizenship Act, 1955?
  Two | *Three | Four | Five
  x: Citizenship is lost in three ways: renunciation, termination and deprivation.
[nead8cf01dab] sub7
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  NRC — all usual residents | NPR — citizens only | *NRC — citizens only | NPR — persons granted citizenship under the CAA alone
  x: The National Register of Citizens concerns citizens only, whereas the National Population Register covers all usual residents. Thus, NRC—citizens only is correctly matched.
[fefc42f2c2d6c] sub7
  Of which of the following is the National Register of Citizens (NRC) a subset?
  *National Population Register | Aadhaar database | Electoral roll | Central OCI database
  x: The NRC covers citizens only and is a subset of the NPR, which lists all usual residents.
[g969a438c3f9d] sub7
  Consider the following statements:
1. The National Register of Citizens (NRC) covers all usual residents of India.
2. A draft NRC has been published for Assam.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | *2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: The NRC lists citizens only (the NPR covers all usual residents); a draft NRC was published for Assam.
[g5b6612ada058] sub7
  Under Section 9 of which Act is it stated that the number issued under it is not a proof of citizenship?
  Passports Act, 1967 | Citizenship Act, 1955 | *Aadhaar Act | Representation of the People Act, 1950
  x: Section 9 of the Aadhaar Act says Aadhaar is proof of identity only, not of citizenship.

=== NOTES 2:8 | Fundamental Rights – Article 12, Article 13 and Doctrines
## SUB 0 | Part III at a Glance – Six Groups, Number, Location
[8:0:0] # Where and how many
[8:0:1] - **Location:** **Part III, Articles 12–35** of the Constitution; called the **"Magna Carta of India"** (not Part I, II or IV; not Arts. 14–32 only).
[8:0:2] - **Number:** originally **seven**, now **six** Fundamental Rights (groups) – the **Right to Property (Art. 31)** was deleted by the **44th Amendment, 1978** (Janata Party government) and is now a **legal/constitutional right under Art. 300A (Part XII)**.
[8:0:3] - **Borrowed from:** the **Bill of Rights of the USA**; the **Motilal Nehru Report (1928)** had demanded a bill of rights.
[8:0:4] - **Why "fundamental":** they are **guaranteed and specifically protected by the Constitution**, the fundamental law of the land – not rights granted by any ordinary law, nor merely natural-law rights.
[8:0:5] # The six groups
[8:0:6] TABLE head: Group | Articles | Contents
  [8:0:6.0] Right to Equality | 14–18 | 14 equality before law; 15 no discrimination (religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth); 16 public employment; 17 untouchability abolished; 18 titles abolished
  [8:0:6.1] Right to Freedom | 19–22 | 19 six freedoms (speech, assembly, association, movement, residence, profession); 20 conviction; 21 life and personal liberty; 21A education; 22 arrest and detention
  [8:0:6.2] Right against Exploitation | 23–24 | 23 traffic in human beings, forced labour; 24 child labour in factories
  [8:0:6.3] Right to Freedom of Religion | 25–28 | conscience, religious affairs, taxes, religious instruction
  [8:0:6.4] Cultural and Educational Rights | 29–30 | minorities' culture; minorities' institutions
  [8:0:6.5] Right to Constitutional Remedies | 32 alone | writs; Art. 226 (High Courts) is outside Part III
[8:0:7] - **Classification traps:** **Art. 15** (prohibition of discrimination) and **Art. 17** (untouchability) fall under the **Right to Equality** – not Freedom of Religion, Exploitation or Cultural rights (UPSC 1995, 2020; SSC 2018).
[8:0:8] - **Wrong pair:** "Right to protection of life – **Art. 20**" is wrong; life and liberty is **Art. 21** (Art. 20 = protection in respect of conviction). Art. 15 discrimination, Art. 19 association and Art. 32 remedies are correct pairs.
[8:0:9] - **Description:** "a pledge to our people and a pact with the civilised world" – **S. Radhakrishnan**.
[8:0:10] > Asked as: "How many FRs?" (six – APPSC Drug Inspectors, Polytechnic Lecturers; SSC CGL 2017), "Which Part?" (III – APPSC G2 2008; SSC 2008, 2017), "Which Articles?" (12–35; Equality 14–18; Freedom 19–22), CDS 2003 "originally seven, now six – both correct".
[8:0:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC Exams (src/polity/nextgen-polity-full.txt) p.169-171; Rocket Polity Part 1 (src/polity/rocket-polity-01.txt) p.38-40; app notes 2:8 sub 0
## SUB 1 | Citizens Only vs All Persons; Aliens
[8:1:0] # Who gets which right
[8:1:1] TABLE head: Citizens only | All persons (citizens + foreigners)
  [8:1:1.0] Art. 15 – no discrimination | Art. 14 – equality before law, equal protection of laws
  [8:1:1.1] Art. 16 – public employment | Art. 20 – protection in respect of conviction
  [8:1:1.2] Art. 19 – six freedoms (speech, movement, trade and profession…) | Art. 21 / 21A – life and liberty, education
  [8:1:1.3] Art. 29 – minorities' language and culture | Art. 22 – arrest safeguards (not enemy aliens)
  [8:1:1.4] Art. 30 – minorities' institutions | Arts. 23–24 – exploitation; Arts. 25–28 – religion; Art. 32 – remedies
[8:1:2] - **Five citizens-only Articles: 15, 16, 19, 29, 30** (Raj. PCS 2024; APPSC G1 2023: denied to aliens = Arts. **29–30, 19, 16** – **not 23–24**).
[8:1:3] - A **British citizen** (any foreigner) in India cannot claim **freedom of trade and profession** or **freedom of speech and expression**, free movement or residence (all Art. 19) – but gets equality before law, life and liberty, religious freedom (UPSC 1999).
[8:1:4] - **Non-citizens** get the **right to life and liberty** and **constitutional remedies** – not freedom of expression or of association (Art. 19).
[8:1:5] - **Aliens** are not given the citizens-only FRs – they are the category to whom FRs are "not given" (bankrupt or sick persons and political sufferers keep all their FRs).
[8:1:6] - Also citizens only: **contesting elections** (not an FR at all).
[8:1:7] - **Aliens:** the Constitution **does distinguish friendly and enemy aliens** – **enemy aliens** lose even the arrest-and-detention safeguards of **Art. 22(1)-(2)**, by **Art. 22(3)**.
[8:1:8] - **Companies** (legal persons) can claim the all-persons rights, but **not Art. 19**.
[8:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.172-173 (citizens-only Arts. 15, 16, 19, 29, 30; all persons 14, 20, 21, 23, 25-28); Rocket Polity Part 1 p.38-39; app notes 2:8 sub 2
## SUB 2 | FR or Not? – Look-alike Rights
[8:2:0] # Not Fundamental Rights
[8:2:1] TABLE head: Right | Where it really is
  [8:2:1.0] Right to work / employment | DPSP Art. 41 (not an FR)
  [8:2:1.1] Equal pay for equal work | DPSP Art. 39(d)
  [8:2:1.2] Right to property | Art. 300A, legal right since the 44th Amendment (1978)
  [8:2:1.3] Right to vote and to be elected | Constitutional right (Art. 326) – UPSC 2017 official key; given effect by statute (RPA 1950 s.19, RPA 1951 s.62); NextGen calls it a statutory right
  [8:2:1.4] Right to information | Not listed in Part III; the Supreme Court reads it into Art. 19(1)(a) (RTI Act 2005 is a statute)
  [8:2:1.5] Right to protest, right to govern, right to citizenship, right of equal shelter | Not FRs (shelter is read into Art. 21 by courts, but no "right of equal shelter" is granted)
[8:2:2] # Look-alikes that ARE Fundamental Rights
[8:2:3] - **Education (6–14 years):** **Art. 21A**, inserted by the **86th Amendment, 2002**; the **RTE Act** was enacted in **2009**; both came into force on **1 April 2010**.
[8:2:4] - **Right to settle in any part of India** – Art. 19(1)(e); **gender equality** – Arts. 14, 15; **form associations**, **practise any profession** – Art. 19.
[8:2:5] - **Right to Equality, Freedom, against Exploitation, Freedom of Religion** – all FR groups (the "odd one out" is always the DPSP or statutory right).
[8:2:6] > NDA 2015: of education, work, associations and profession, only the **right to work** is not an FR. Voting age was lowered to **18** by the **61st Amendment (1988)**.
[8:2:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.170-172, p.174 (vote: statutory right, RPA s.62); upsc.gov.in answer key CSP 2017 (constitutional right); app notes 2:8 sub 7
## SUB 3 | Features and Nature of Fundamental Rights
[8:3:0] # Features
[8:3:1] - **Justiciable:** a person can move the **Supreme Court directly (Art. 32)** and **High Courts (Art. 226)**; FRs are **above ordinary laws** (Art. 13).
[8:3:2] - **Not absolute but qualified:** the State can impose **reasonable restrictions** by law; the **courts judge reasonableness**; **Art. 19** lists **11 grounds** of restriction in all.
[8:3:3] - **Claimed against the State:** most FRs are available against **arbitrary State action** and **limit the power of the State – even Parliament**; some (15(2), 17, 23, 24) also bind private persons.
[8:3:4] - **Not sacrosanct:** Parliament can curtail them **only by a constitutional amendment** (not an ordinary law), without destroying the **basic structure**; they can be **suspended only as the Constitution prescribes** (Arts. 358-359).
[8:3:5] - **Negative and positive:** many are **negatively worded** (14, 15(1), 16(2), 18(1), 20, 22(1), 28(1)); **21A, 29, 30** confer privileges; **15, 17, 18, 20, 24** are absolute limits on legislative power.
[8:3:6] - **Both natural and legal**; they protect **individuals and minority groups** from arbitrary State action (both statements correct).
[8:3:7] - **Britain** has **no written FRs**; it relies on conventions and the rule of law.
[8:3:8] # Statement traps
[8:3:9] - **Wrong:** "FRs are **unamendable**", "**absolute**", "partly justiciable and absolute", "not limited by the Constitution", "all FRs are suspended in an emergency".
[8:3:10] - **Right:** "not absolute", "some only for citizens", "drawn from the American Constitution", "justiciable", "six in number", "granted by the Constitution".
[8:3:11] - **CDS 2004:** FRs are fundamental because they are protected and enforced by the Constitution, can be suspended only as it prescribes, and are enforceable against the State (I, II, III) – **not** because they cannot be amended.
[8:3:12] - **A–R (UPPSC 2015):** the Constitution is the longest (A true) and the FR chapter follows the American model (R true), but R does not explain A.
[8:3:13] - **Right to die:** **Gian Kaur (1996)** – no right to die (suicide) under Art. 21; **Common Cause (2018)** – right to **die with dignity** (passive euthanasia, living will) is part of Art. 21.
[8:3:14] - **India is not a fully federal constitution** – it is federal with a unitary bias ("quasi-federal").
[8:3:15] - **Golden Triangle:** **Arts. 14, 19 and 21** (Maneka Gandhi 1978; Minerva Mills 1980) – not 14-20-21, 14-15-16, 19-20-21 or 14-21-32.
[8:3:16] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.169-173 (features), p.200 (Golden Triangle 14, 19, 21); Rocket Polity Part 1 p.38-40, Part 1 p.59 (Maneka Gandhi: golden triangle); sci.gov.in (Common Cause v. UoI, 9 Mar 2018); app notes 2:8 sub 1
## SUB 4 | Limits on FRs – Restrictions, Armed Forces (Arts. 33-35), Emergency (358-359)
[8:4:0] # Who can restrict
[8:4:1] - **Reasonable restrictions** are imposed by **law** made by **Parliament** (and State legislatures within their field) – not by the President, Prime Minister or Supreme Court; the courts only **judge** their reasonableness.
[8:4:2] # Articles 33–35
[8:4:3] TABLE head: Article | Provision
  [8:4:3.0] 33 | Parliament alone may restrict or abrogate FRs of the armed forces, forces charged with maintenance of public order (police), intelligence/counter-intelligence staff, and persons in telecommunication systems set up for those forces – including non-combatants (barbers, cooks, carpenters). Examples: Army Act 1950, Air Force Act 1950, Navy Act 1957
  [8:4:3.1] 34 | Restrictions while martial law is in force; Parliament may pass an indemnity Act; "martial law" is not defined in the Constitution
  [8:4:3.2] 35 | Only Parliament (not States) legislates on 16(3), 32(3), 33, 34 and punishments for Part III offences – for uniformity
[8:4:4] - **CDS 2015 trap:** staff of **communication systems set up in the country in general** are **not** covered by Art. 33 – they enjoy FRs at par with other citizens; the armed forces, public-order forces and **their** communication staff are the restricted categories.
[8:4:5] # Emergency
[8:4:6] - **Art. 358:** when a National Emergency is declared on **war or external aggression**, **Art. 19 (Right to Freedom) is suspended automatically** (not on armed rebellion).
[8:4:7] - **Art. 359:** the **President** may by order suspend the right to move courts for **specified FRs** – **never Arts. 20 and 21** (**44th Amendment, 1978**).
[8:4:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.172 (Art. 19 suspension), p.223-225 (Arts. 33-35, 358-359); Rocket Polity Part 1 p.40; app notes 2:8 sub 6
## SUB 5 | Article 13 – Laws Void, Meaning of "Law", Amendments
[8:5:0] # Clauses
[8:5:1] TABLE head: Clause | Provision
  [8:5:1.0] 13(1) | Pre-constitution laws inconsistent with FRs are void to the extent of inconsistency
  [8:5:1.1] 13(2) | The State shall not make any law that takes away or abridges FRs; such post-constitution laws are void to that extent
  [8:5:1.2] 13(3)(a) | "Law" includes ordinance, order, bye-law, rule, regulation, notification, custom or usage having the force of law
  [8:5:1.3] 13(4) | Art. 13 does not apply to Art. 368 amendments – added by the 24th Amendment, 1971
[8:5:2] - **Basis of judicial review** (with Arts. 32 and 226); it gives **constitutional provisions (FRs) precedence over laws** of Parliament and State legislatures.
[8:5:3] - 13(1) refers to laws in force before the **commencement of the Constitution** (26 Jan 1950), not "before Independence".
[8:5:4] - **UPSC 2026:** "law" in Art. 13 includes **custom or usage** – so the view that custom is included is correct and the view excluding it is wrong (only Y correct).
[8:5:5] # Are amendments "law"?
[8:5:6] TABLE head: Case / Amendment | Year | Position
  [8:5:6.0] Shankari Prasad | 1951 | Amendment is not "law" under Art. 13; Parliament can amend FRs (first such case)
  [8:5:6.1] Golaknath | 1967 | FRs have a transcendental position – Parliament cannot abridge FRs; prospective overruling
  [8:5:6.2] 24th Amendment | 1971 | Added Art. 13(4) in reply
  [8:5:6.3] Kesavananda Bharati | 1973 | FRs amendable, but the basic structure cannot be destroyed
[8:5:7] - **Ninth Schedule:** added by the **1st Amendment (1951)** with **Arts. 31A and 31B**; **I.R. Coelho (2007)** – laws placed in it after 24 Apr 1973 are open to basic-structure review.
[8:5:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.179-182 (Art. 13 clauses, 13(4), Golaknath); Rocket Polity Part 1 p.38; app notes 2:8 sub 4, sub 9
## SUB 6 | Doctrines under Article 13
[8:6:0] TABLE head: Doctrine | Meaning | Case
  [8:6:0.0] Severability | Law is void only to the extent of inconsistency; the consistent, severable part remains valid | A.K. Gopalan (1950); R.M.D.C. (1957); Motor General Traders v. State of AP (1984)
  [8:6:0.1] Eclipse | A pre-constitution law inconsistent with an FR is not wiped out – it is dormant (overshadowed) and revives when the conflict is removed | Bhikaji Narain Dhakras v. State of MP (1955); State of Gujarat v. Ambica Mills (1974) – extended to post-constitution laws for non-citizens
  [8:6:0.2] Waiver | A person cannot waive an FR | Basheshar Nath v. CIT (1959)
  [8:6:0.3] Prospective overruling | A ruling operates only for the future | Golaknath (1967)
  [8:6:0.4] Progressive interpretation | Constitution as a living document | Maneka Gandhi (1978) widened Art. 21; SaveLIFE Foundation v. UoI (2026) – right to trauma care under Art. 21
  [8:6:0.5] Absolute liability | Hazardous industry liable without exceptions | M.C. Mehta (1987) – Oleum gas leak
  [8:6:0.6] Territorial nexus | State law can operate outside the State if there is sufficient connection | State of Bombay v. R.M.D.C. (1957)
  [8:6:0.7] Pith and substance | Encroachment on another List – look at the true nature of the law | Federal doctrine (with aspect, colourable legislation, incidental and ancillary powers, repugnancy)
[8:6:1] - **Eclipse vs severability:** eclipse = law **sleeps and revives**; severability = **bad part cut off, rest lives**. Eminent domain and pleasure are unrelated doctrines.
[8:6:2] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.180-182 (doctrines; Motor General Traders 1984; Ambica Mills 1974); app notes 2:8 sub 5
## SUB 7 | Article 12 – Meaning of "the State"
[8:7:0] # Definition
[8:7:1] - **Government and Parliament of India**; **Government and Legislature of each State**; **all local authorities** (municipalities, panchayats, district boards); **other authorities** within India or under the control of the Government of India (LIC, ONGC, electricity boards).
[8:7:2] - **Ambiguity – courts:** whether the **judiciary** is "other authority" is the grey area; in its judicial function it is **not** "State" (**Naresh Mirajkar v. State of Maharashtra, 1967**).
[8:7:3] - **Private bodies:** a writ does not ordinarily lie merely because a private body performs a public activity, unless the State is involved.
[8:7:4] TABLE head: Case | Year | Holding
  [8:7:4.0] Rajasthan SEB v. Mohan Lal | 1967 | Statutory bodies are "other authorities"
  [8:7:4.1] R.D. Shetty v. International Airport Authority | 1979 | Laid down tests for an instrumentality or agency of the State
  [8:7:4.2] Ajay Hasia v. Khalid Mujib | 1981 | Society running a Regional Engineering College = State; test is creation and control, not legal form
  [8:7:4.3] Pradeep Kumar Biswas | 2002 | State if financially, functionally and administratively dominated by government
[8:7:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.174-175, p.178-179 (Naresh 1967; other authorities); app notes 2:8 sub 3
## SUB 8 | Violation Scenarios
[8:8:0] TABLE head: Scenario | Article violated
  [8:8:0.0] Not paying minimum wages | Art. 23 – forced labour (PUDR / Asiad workers, 1982)
  [8:8:0.1] Child below 14 in a factory or mine | Art. 24
  [8:8:0.2] Admission denied in a State-aided institution on religion, race, caste, language | Art. 29(2)
  [8:8:0.3] Dalits kept from a public well | Art. 15(2)
  [8:8:0.4] Dalits kept from a public shop | Art. 17
  [8:8:0.5] Banning an association | Art. 19(4) – valid only on permitted grounds
  [8:8:0.6] Arts. 19/21 violated by a private individual | Ordinary legal remedy, not a writ
[8:8:1] - **Not violations (reasonable restrictions):** **banning secessionist activities** (sovereignty and integrity, 16th Amendment 1963), **no loudspeakers in a religious procession** (public order, health – Church of God, 2000); a **book ban** is valid if it falls within Art. 19(2) grounds.
[8:8:2] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity p.173 (private violation: legal remedy); PUDR v. UoI (1982) 3 SCC 235; app notes 2:8 sub 8

=== OWN MCQs of 2:8 (76)
[n70472205d6f] sub0
  Which one of the following correctly represents the present number and original number of groups of Fundamental Rights?
  Presently seven; originally six | Presently five; originally six | *Presently six; originally seven | Presently six; originally five
  x: The Constitution originally contained seven groups of Fundamental Rights. The right to property was removed from Part III by the 44th Amendment, leaving six groups at present.
[n42d8e5bc4cb] sub0
  After the 44th Amendment, 1978, under which Article is the right to property protected?
  Article 31 | Article 19(1)(f) | Article 39(d) | *Article 300A
  x: The 44th Amendment removed property from Part III; it is now a constitutional/legal right under Article 300A in Part XII.
[n0095ea31a71] sub0
  Where are the Fundamental Rights, often described as the 'Magna Carta of India', contained?
  *Part III, Articles 12 to 35 | Part IV, Articles 36 to 51 | Part III, Articles 14 to 32 only | Part IVA, Article 51A
  x: Fundamental Rights are in Part III, Articles 12 to 35 (Art. 12 defines State, Art. 13 voids inconsistent laws, Arts. 33-35 cover limits and legislation). Part IV (Arts. 36-51) holds the Directive Principles and Part IVA (Art. 51A) the Fundamental Duties; Arts. 14-32 is only the span of the six groups, not the whole Part.
[n72588981b63] sub0
  Which Articles contain the right to freedom of religion?
  23 to 24 | 14 to 18 | 29 to 30 | *25 to 28
  x: Freedom of religion is in Articles 25-28; equality is 14-18, exploitation 23-24 and cultural and educational rights 29-30.
[nindian-polity-15-4] sub0
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  Right to Equality – Articles 25–28 | Right to Freedom of Religion – Article 32 | *Right against Exploitation – Articles 23–24 | Right to Constitutional Remedies – Articles 14–18
  x: The Right against Exploitation is Articles 23–24; Equality is 14–18, Freedom of Religion 25–28 and Constitutional Remedies Article 32.
[f4a38e461bd02] sub0
  Which Articles make up the Right to Constitutional Remedies?
  Articles 30 to 32 | *Article 32 alone | Articles 32 to 35 | Articles 32 and 226
  x: The Right to Constitutional Remedies is Article 32 alone, not Articles 30-32.
[f0f901bc81860] sub0
  Who described Fundamental Rights as 'a pledge to our people and a pact with the civilised world'?
  B.R. Ambedkar | Jawaharlal Nehru | *S. Radhakrishnan | Rajendra Prasad
  x: The description is attributed to S. Radhakrishnan.
[nindian-polity-16-2] sub0
  Which of the following statements is incorrect?
  Part III of the Constitution contains Articles 12 to 35. | The Right against Exploitation covers Articles 23 to 24. | *The Right to Constitutional Remedies covers Articles 30 to 32. | The Right to Equality covers Articles 14 to 18.
  x: C is incorrect: the Right to Constitutional Remedies is Article 32 alone; Articles 29–30 are cultural and educational rights. Part III (12–35), exploitation (23–24) and equality (14–18) are stated correctly.
[g4a65655abb18] sub0
  Under which group of Fundamental Rights does Article 17 (abolition of untouchability) fall?
  *Right to Equality | Right against Exploitation | Right to Freedom | Cultural and Educational Rights
  x: Article 17, like Article 15, falls under the Right to Equality (Articles 14-18).
[nindian-polity-15-5] sub1
  Consider the following statements:
I. Articles 15, 16, 19, 29 and 30 are available only to citizens.
II. Articles 14, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24 and 25–28 are available to both citizens and foreigners.
III. Right to Property continues to be a Fundamental Right under Article 300-A.
Which of the statements given above are correct?
  *I and II only | II and III only | I, II and III | I and III only
  x: I and II are correct: Articles 15, 16, 19, 29 and 30 are for citizens only, and the others (14, 20–28) extend to foreigners. III is wrong: the 44th Amendment (1978) removed the Right to Property from Part III; Article 300A makes it only a legal right.
[n02dfd0d1cec] sub1
  Which one of the following Articles is available to both citizens and foreigners, subject to constitutional exceptions?
  *Article 21 | Article 19 | Article 16 | Article 29
  x: Article 21 is available to all persons. Articles 19, 16 and 29 are included among the rights available only to citizens.
[n5f06104fe66] sub1
  By which clause of Article 22 are enemy aliens excluded from its safeguards?
  22(1) | *22(3) | 22(5) | 22(7)
  x: Article 22(3) excludes enemy aliens from the specified safeguards; Articles 23 and 24 are not denied to aliens.
[f9fac1550590f] sub1
  Companies can claim the Fundamental Rights available to all persons, but NOT the rights under which Article?
  Article 14 | *Article 19 | Article 20 | Article 32
  x: Article 19 is for citizens only, so companies cannot claim it.
[f2a42beca7bc2] sub1
  Which of the following Fundamental Rights is denied to aliens?
  Article 23 | Article 24 | *Article 29 | Article 21
  x: Articles 29-30, 19 and 16 are denied to aliens; Articles 21, 23 and 24 are available to all persons.
[n9b8b782da34] sub2
  Which one of the following is NOT a Fundamental Right expressly included in Part III?
  Right to freedom of religion | *Right to employment | Right to constitutional remedies | Right to education for children aged six to fourteen years
  x: The right to employment is a Directive Principle under Article 41, not a Fundamental Right. Education for children aged six to fourteen is a Fundamental Right under Article 21A.
[n00969d95e0e] sub2
  Under which Article is 'equal pay for equal work' a constitutional goal?
  39(a) | 41 | *39(d) | 43
  x: Equal pay for equal work is under Article 39(d); property is protected under Article 300A.
[ndb639d94b71] sub2
  Under which Article and Amendment is the right to education for children aged six to fourteen a Fundamental Right?
  Article 21A – 42nd Amendment, 1976 | *Article 21A – 86th Amendment, 2002 | Article 24 – 86th Amendment, 2002 | Article 30 – 73rd Amendment, 1992
  x: Article 21A was inserted by the 86th Amendment Act, 2002; the RTE Act, 2009 gives effect to it.
[nd05298c39ba] sub2
  What is the present status of the right to vote in India?
  A Fundamental Right under Article 19 | A right enforceable only under Article 32 | A Fundamental Right under Article 21 | *A statutory right under Section 62 of the RP Act, 1951
  x: The right to vote is statutory under Section 62 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, with a constitutional basis in Article 326; it is not a Fundamental Right.
[fbd384745ade3] sub2
  Under which Article is the right to work or employment a Directive Principle?
  39(d) | *41 | 21A | 300A
  x: Employment is not a Fundamental Right; it is a DPSP under Article 41, while equal pay for equal work is Article 39(d).
[fcef9a512f431] sub2
  Which Amendment lowered the voting age to 18 years?
  42nd Amendment | 44th Amendment | *61st Amendment | 86th Amendment
  x: The 61st Amendment (1988) fixed the voting age at 18 under Article 326.
[g1bd39ce00e61] sub2
  In which year was the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, which gives effect to Article 21A, enacted?
  2002 | 2005 | *2009 | 2010
  x: Article 21A came by the 86th Amendment (2002) and the RTE Act giving effect to it was passed in 2009.
[ge92b5bc9735a] sub2
  From which date did Article 21A and the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act come into force?
  26 January 2002 | 12 December 2002 | 26 August 2009 | *1 April 2010
  x: The RTE Act was passed in 2009, but Article 21A and the Act came into effect on 1 April 2010.
[g9f810143b2f9] sub2
  Consider the following statements:
1. The Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act was enacted in 2009.
2. Article 21A and the RTE Act came into force in 2010.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | 2 only | *Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: The RTE Act is of 2009, and both Article 21A and the Act came into effect on 1 April 2010.
[g085fa3f16128] sub3
  Consider the following statements about Part III of the Constitution:
1. It is based mainly on the Bill of Rights of the USA.
2. Britain also has a written chapter of Fundamental Rights.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Part III is borrowed mainly from the US Bill of Rights; Britain has no written Fundamental Rights and relies on conventions and the rule of law.
[nc19b9987588] sub3
  Unlike India, through what does Britain largely protect rights?
  A codified chapter of Fundamental Rights | A written Constitution | *Conventions and the rule of law | Directive Principles
  x: Britain has no codified Fundamental Rights; protection rests largely on conventions and the rule of law.
[nac53de68dc5] sub3
  Which one of the following sets consists only of examples of positive Fundamental Rights?
  *Articles 21A, 29 and 30 | Articles 16(2), 17 and 24 | Articles 14, 15(1) and 18(1) | Articles 20, 22(1) and 28(1)
  x: Articles 21A, 29 and 30 confer educational or cultural privileges and are treated as examples of positive rights. The other sets contain negative rights that primarily restrict State action.
[n6cd3ac15e37] sub3
  Which of the following is true of Fundamental Rights in India?
  Absolute and cannot be regulated by law | Enforceable only against private persons | Non-justiciable | *Qualified and subject to reasonable restrictions
  x: Fundamental Rights are qualified, not absolute; courts review restrictions for reasonableness.
[f744dcf19ca01] sub3
  How many grounds of restriction does Article 19 list in all?
  6 | 8 | *11 | 12
  x: Article 19 lists 11 restriction grounds in all.
[fd845e1604502] sub3
  Who judges the reasonableness of restrictions imposed on Fundamental Rights?
  Parliament | *The courts | The President | The Union Cabinet
  x: Courts judge reasonableness through judicial review under Articles 13, 32 and 226.
[f3405087eb670] sub3
  Which of the following Articles also binds private persons?
  Article 14 | *Article 17 | Article 20 | Article 22(1)
  x: Articles 15(2), 17, 23 and 24 also bind private persons.
[g8698ff698024] sub3
  Consider the following statements about Fundamental Rights:
1. They are both natural and legal rights.
2. They protect individuals and minorities.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | 2 only | *Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Fundamental Rights are both natural and legal and protect individuals and minorities (APPSC 2023: both correct).
[n3c91863b517] sub3
  How can Parliament amend a Fundamental Right?
  Through an ordinary law | Not at all | *Only by a constitutional amendment, subject to the basic structure | Only through State Legislatures
  x: Fundamental Rights can be altered only by the amendment procedure, subject to the basic-structure doctrine of Kesavananda Bharati (1973); an ordinary law cannot amend the Constitution.
[n804cfd4f032] sub4
  Under Article 33, who has the power to restrict the Fundamental Rights of members of the armed forces?
  The President | State Legislatures | *Parliament | The Supreme Court
  x: Article 33 gives the power exclusively to Parliament, and it may extend to non-combatant employees such as cooks and barbers.
[n327b6e97c61] sub4
  During a National Emergency, the President's order under Article 359 cannot suspend the enforcement of which of the following Articles, as provided by the 44th Amendment, 1978?
  Articles 14 and 19 | *Articles 20 and 21 | Articles 21 and 32 | Articles 19 and 22
  x: Since the 44th Amendment (1978), enforcement of Arts. 20 and 21 can never be suspended under Art. 359. Arts. 14, 19, 22 and the right to move courts under Art. 32 for other rights can be affected by such an order, so the other pairs are wrong.
[n79471b49dc2] sub4
  Which Article provides for restriction of Fundamental Rights while martial law is in force?
  Article 33 | *Article 34 | Article 35 | Article 359
  x: Article 34 concerns restrictions during martial law and indemnity by Parliament; Article 33 deals with the armed forces.
[f96720666a013] sub4
  Under Article 35, who has the power to legislate on Articles 16(3), 32(3), 33 and 34?
  State Legislatures | *Parliament only | Both Parliament and State Legislatures | The President
  x: Article 35 gives this power to Parliament alone, for uniformity.
[f9a03be850189] sub4
  Consider the following statements about Article 34:
1. It allows restrictions on Fundamental Rights while martial law is in force.
2. Parliament may pass an indemnity Act.
3. The Constitution defines 'martial law'.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 and 2 only | 2 and 3 only | 1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: Statements 1 and 2 are correct; 'martial law' is not defined in the Constitution.
[naee50e62ea5] sub5
  Which of the following is included within the expression ‘law’ under Article 13(3)?
  *Ordinance, order, bye-law, rule, regulation, notification, custom or usage | Only a constitutional amendment made under Article 368 | Only a judicial decision of the Supreme Court | Only an Act passed by Parliament
  x: Article 13(3) gives a broad meaning to law, including ordinances, orders, bye-laws, rules, regulations, notifications, customs and usages having the force of law.
[n7ad0cee72cf] sub5
  How are constitutional amendments made under Article 368 treated under Article 13(4)?
  Treated as ordinary laws under Article 13 | Void if they abridge any Fundamental Right | Valid only for State subjects | *Not treated as 'law' under Article 13
  x: Article 13(4), inserted by the 24th Amendment, says Article 13 does not apply to amendments made under Article 368.
[n41b73c525d4] sub5
  Article 13(1) makes void, to the extent of inconsistency with Fundamental Rights, laws in force before which event?
  Independence | *The commencement of the Constitution | The First Amendment | The 24th Amendment
  x: Article 13(1) covers laws in force before the commencement of the Constitution, not 'before Independence'.
[n5491751c43a] sub5
  In which case did the Supreme Court hold that Fundamental Rights occupy a transcendental position and that Parliament cannot abridge them, a position later countered by the 24th Amendment (1971)?
  Kesavananda Bharati (1973) | *Golaknath (1967) | Shankari Prasad (1951) | Maneka Gandhi (1978)
  x: Golaknath (1967) gave FRs a transcendental position and held Parliament could not abridge them; the 24th Amendment (1971) responded by adding Art. 13(4). Kesavananda Bharati (1973) came after and laid down the basic structure; Shankari Prasad had upheld Parliament's power to amend FRs; Maneka Gandhi (1978) widened Art. 21.
[f4a00c9de3b95] sub5
  In which case did the Supreme Court first hold that a constitutional amendment is not 'law' under Article 13?
  Golaknath | *Shankari Prasad | Kesavananda Bharati | I.R. Coelho
  x: Shankari Prasad (1951) held an amendment is not 'law'; Golaknath (1967) took the opposite view.
[faeb1f6990ef6] sub5
  Which Amendment added Article 13(4)?
  1st Amendment, 1951 | 44th Amendment, 1978 | *24th Amendment, 1971 | 86th Amendment, 2002
  x: The 24th Amendment (1971) added Article 13(4) in reply to Golaknath.
[n57c1ae9d039] sub5
  Which 2007 decision limited the protection given by the Ninth Schedule?
  Minerva Mills | Kesavananda Bharati | *I.R. Coelho | Waman Rao
  x: I.R. Coelho (2007) limited Ninth Schedule protection; the Schedule itself came with the 1st Amendment (1951).
[fc1fef3af2604] sub5
  Which Amendment added the Ninth Schedule to the Constitution?
  *1st Amendment, 1951 | 24th Amendment, 1971 | 44th Amendment, 1978 | 86th Amendment, 2002
  x: The 1st Amendment (1951) added Articles 31A, 31B and the Ninth Schedule.
[g627b2059719d] sub5
  Which Articles were added to the Constitution by the 1st Amendment, 1951?
  *Articles 31A and 31B | Articles 21A and 51A | Articles 32A and 226A | Articles 31C and 39A
  x: The 1st Amendment (1951) added Articles 31A and 31B and the Ninth Schedule.
[nc5324331ff7] sub6
  Which case is associated with the widening of Article 21 through progressive interpretation?
  *Maneka Gandhi v. Union of India | Shankari Prasad v. Union of India | Basheshar Nath v. CIT | Bhikaji Narain Dhakras v. State of MP
  x: Maneka Gandhi (1978) widened the interpretation of Article 21 and is an important example of progressive constitutional interpretation.
[na221c3d8295] sub6
  Which case is associated with the doctrine of absolute liability for hazardous industries?
  Golaknath | M.C. Mehta v. Kamal Nath | Motor General Traders v. State of Andhra Pradesh | *M.C. Mehta, Oleum gas leak case
  x: The absolute liability principle was evolved in M.C. Mehta’s 1987 Oleum gas leak case. It imposes liability on hazardous industries without the exceptions available under strict liability.
[n9328d511fd3] sub6
  Which doctrine is most directly applicable when a law enacted by one legislature appears to encroach upon a subject in another legislative List?
  Prospective overruling | Absolute liability | Public trust | *Pith and substance
  x: Pith and substance is the relevant doctrine for examining Union-State List encroachment by looking at the true nature and character of the law.
[nc9657ec54a8] sub6
  Which of the following pairs of doctrine and meaning is wrongly matched?
  *Eclipse – Only the inconsistent portion of a law is void | Waiver – A person cannot give up a Fundamental Right | Prospective overruling – Ruling operates only for the future | Severability – Only the inconsistent portion of a law is void
  x: Eclipse means an inconsistent law stays dormant and may revive; voiding only the inconsistent portion is severability.
[nf3ca3525db4] sub6
  Which one of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
  Waiver — Basheshar Nath case | Prospective overruling — Golaknath case | Eclipse — Bhikaji Narain Dhakras case | *Severability — State of Gujarat v. Ambica Mills
  x: State of Gujarat v. Ambica Mills is associated with the extension of the doctrine of Eclipse to post-Constitution laws affecting non-citizens. Severability is associated with cases such as A.K. Gopalan, R.M.D.C. and Motor General Traders.
[f4f8692c80c4c] sub6
  Under the doctrine of eclipse, what happens to a law inconsistent with Fundamental Rights?
  Is void from the beginning and can never revive | *Becomes dormant and revives when the conflict is removed | Remains valid except for the inconsistent part | Can be waived by the affected citizen
  x: Under eclipse the law is dormant, not dead, and revives when the conflict is removed.
[f2d03d53a3411] sub6
  The doctrine of eclipse was applied in which case?
  Basheshar Nath v. CIT | A.K. Gopalan | M.C. Mehta | *Bhikaji Narain Dhakras v. State of MP
  x: In Bhikaji Narain Dhakras (1955) the law revived after the 1st Amendment; Basheshar Nath is the waiver case.
[f80dfdc933156] sub6
  What did the Supreme Court hold in Basheshar Nath v. CIT (1959)?
  Only the inconsistent part of a law is void | A pre-constitution law revives after amendment | *A person cannot waive a Fundamental Right | Rulings operate only for the future
  x: Basheshar Nath laid down that a Fundamental Right cannot be waived.
[f13ebde624417] sub6
  The doctrine of prospective overruling is associated with which case?
  Maneka Gandhi | Rajasthan SEB v. Mohan Lal | *Golaknath | Kesavananda Bharati
  x: Golaknath (1967) applied prospective overruling; 1967 is also the year of Rajasthan SEB, an Article 12 case.
[g912cbca03ddf] sub6
  In which case was the doctrine of eclipse extended to post-constitution laws in respect of non-citizens?
  Bhikaji Narain Dhakras v. State of MP | *State of Gujarat v. Ambica Mills | State of Bombay v. R.M.D.C. | Golaknath v. State of Punjab
  x: Ambica Mills (1974) applied eclipse to post-constitution laws for non-citizens; Bhikaji Narain (1955) applied it to a pre-constitution law.
[g7268a95a5c26] sub6
  Under which doctrine can a State law operate outside the State if there is a sufficient connection, as held in State of Bombay v. R.M.D.C. (1957)?
  Pith and substance | *Territorial nexus | Colourable legislation | Eclipse
  x: The doctrine of territorial nexus allows a State law to apply outside the State when there is sufficient connection.
[g39684310868a] sub6
  In which of the following cases was the doctrine of severability applied?
  Basheshar Nath v. CIT, 1959 | Bhikaji Narain Dhakras v. State of MP, 1955 | Golaknath v. State of Punjab, 1967 | *A.K. Gopalan v. State of Madras, 1950
  x: Severability (only the inconsistent part is void) was applied in A.K. Gopalan (1950) and R.M.D.C. (1957).
[g2ce2b274de8d] sub6
  In SaveLIFE Foundation v. Union of India (2026), which right did the Supreme Court read into Article 21?
  Right to vote | *Right to trauma care | Right to employment | Right to property
  x: Through progressive interpretation the Court held trauma care to be part of the right to life under Article 21.
[gac35f80fad89] sub6
  A law inconsistent with Fundamental Rights is void only to the extent of the inconsistency, and the rest of it remains valid. Which doctrine is this?
  Doctrine of eclipse | *Doctrine of severability | Doctrine of waiver | Doctrine of pith and substance
  x: Under the doctrine of severability only the inconsistent part is void; the rest survives if it can be separated.
[geb441f00a926] sub6
  With which doctrine is the case Motor General Traders v. State of Andhra Pradesh (1984) associated?
  Eclipse | Waiver | *Severability | Territorial nexus
  x: The notes list A.K. Gopalan, R.M.D.C. and Motor General Traders v. State of AP among the severability cases.
[nindian-polity-15-19] sub7
  Which Article defines the 'State' against which Fundamental Rights are available?
  *Article 12 | Article 13 | Article 32 | Article 35
  x: Fundamental Rights are enforceable against the State, and Article 12 defines which bodies count as the 'State'.
[n180c8d03fa4] sub7
  Which of the following is NOT correct about 'State' under Article 12?
  It includes the Government and Parliament of India. | It includes the Government and Legislature of each State. | It includes other authorities in India or under the control of the Government of India. | *It excludes local authorities.
  x: Article 12 includes all local authorities within 'State'.
[n91cfc8e8c72] sub7
  Which of the following is NOT correct about Ajay Hasia v. Khalid Mujib?
  A society running a Regional Engineering College was treated as 'State'. | *The test depends merely on the legal form of the body. | The test looks at how the body was created and controlled. | It was decided in 1981.
  x: In Ajay Hasia (1981) the Court stressed creation and control of the body, not merely its formal legal form.
[nbfc687366d8] sub7
  Which case held that statutory bodies are 'other authorities' under Article 12?
  R.D. Shetty v. International Airport Authority | Ajay Hasia v. Khalid Mujib | *Rajasthan SEB v. Mohan Lal | Pradeep Kumar Biswas
  x: Rajasthan SEB v. Mohan Lal (1967) held statutory bodies to be 'other authorities'; R.D. Shetty (1979) laid down tests for an instrumentality of the State.
[n3acc56922eb] sub7
  A private organisation performs a public activity, but no governmental authority is shown to be involved in the alleged violation. Which position is most consistent with the general rule concerning writs?
  A writ can never lie against a private organisation under any circumstances | A writ ordinarily lies because every public activity makes the organisation ‘State’ | *A writ does not ordinarily lie merely because the organisation performs a public activity | A writ lies only when Parliament specifically authorises the organisation
  x: Writs do not ordinarily lie against private bodies merely on the basis of their public activity. The rule is not absolute, since State collusion can alter the position.
[f282ac4a957c5] sub7
  In Pradeep Kumar Biswas (2002), on what basis was a body held to be 'State'?
  Created by a statute | Performing any public activity | Registered as a society | *Financially, functionally and administratively dominated by the government
  x: Pradeep Kumar Biswas laid down the test of financial, functional and administrative domination by the government.
[g52e0cf3ea2d4] sub7
  Which case laid down the tests for deciding whether a body is an instrumentality or agency of the State under Article 12?
  Rajasthan SEB v. Mohan Lal | *R.D. Shetty v. International Airport Authority | Basheshar Nath v. CIT | A.K. Gopalan v. State of Madras
  x: R.D. Shetty v. International Airport Authority (1979) laid down the tests for an instrumentality or agency of the State.
[nce850a41547] sub8
  On what basis can a prohibition imposed on an association be treated as a restriction under Article 19(4)?
  *The grounds constitutionally permitted under Article 19(4) | A private contractual dispute alone | Any reason considered desirable by the executive | An ordinance that is immune from judicial review
  x: Restrictions on freedom of association under Article 19(4) must be based on the constitutionally specified grounds and remain subject to constitutional scrutiny.
[nbd0942f5af4] sub8
  Excluding Dalits from a public shop may violate which Article?
  Article 23 | *Article 17 | Article 24 | Article 21A
  x: Exclusion of Dalits from a public shop attracts Article 17 (abolition of untouchability), which also operates against private persons.
[n5256274c1e9] sub8
  Paying labourers less than the minimum wage violates which Article?
  *23 | 24 | 29(2) | 17
  x: Paying less than minimum wage is forced labour under Art. 23 (PUDR/Asiad, 1982); child labour in mines violates Art. 24.
[f1c84ad5f43d6] sub8
  Employing a child below 14 in a factory or mine violates which Article?
  Article 23 | *Article 24 | Article 21A | Article 29(2)
  x: Article 24 prohibits employment of children below 14 in factories and mines.
[fe0c5b38826be] sub8
  Denying admission in a State-aided institution on grounds of religion, race, caste or language violates which Article?
  Article 17 | Article 23 | Article 30(1) | *Article 29(2)
  x: Article 29(2) bars such denial of admission in State-aided institutions.
[f283976520fe9] sub8
  What is the remedy when Article 19 or 21 rights are violated by a private individual?
  A writ under Article 32 | A writ under Article 226 | *An ordinary legal remedy, not a writ | A Presidential order under Article 359
  x: Against a private individual violating Articles 19 or 21, the remedy is an ordinary legal one, not a writ.
[g221d1186bc5a] sub8
  In which case was payment below the minimum wage held to be forced labour under Article 23?
  M.C. Mehta case, 1987 | Maneka Gandhi case, 1978 | *PUDR (Asiad workers) case, 1982 | Ajay Hasia case, 1981
  x: In the PUDR (Asiad) case of 1982, paying less than the minimum wage was held to be forced labour under Article 23.
[ga2d6e1c9476c] sub8
  Keeping Dalits away from a public well may violate which Article of the Constitution?
  Article 23 | *Article 15(2) | Article 29(2) | Article 24
  x: Article 15(2) bars denial of access to public wells, tanks and places on grounds such as caste.
